CHAPTER 106 â€” THE FIRST TASK

The key to the reading room opened the door at the top of the stair above the chandler's shop.

I went there on the first day of my working relationship, in the evening, when the shop below was shut and the stair was dark. I climbed to the top, and I put the key in the lock, and the door opened.

The room was as I had left it â€” bare, with a single lamp on a table in the middle of the floor. The three sheets of paper were gone. In their place, on the table, lay a single sheet, folded once, in a hand I was beginning to know.

I crossed the room. I opened the paper.

*Mr. Ashcroft,*

*There is a book in the possession of a man named Silas Wren, of this town. He keeps it among a collection of objects that have no history â€” objects from which the origin has been deliberately removed.*

*We have been trying to acquire this book for some time. The owner will not sell it. He will not show it. He will not say where he obtained it.*

*We do not ask you to steal it. We do not ask you to take it by force. We ask you to find out what is in it â€” and, if you can, to learn how it came into his possession.*

*Your discretion is assumed.*

There was no signature.

I read the paper twice. Then I folded it and put it in my coat.

Silas Wren.

I had heard the name â€” from Ysabel, in the weeks after the clock, when she had spoken of a collector who kept objects without history. Coins with no year. Books with no author. Paintings with no signature. She had said: "Someone deliberately erased their origin."

Silas Wren was that collector.

And he had a book that the society wanted.

I could not yet tell what was in the book. I could not yet tell how it had come into his possession. I could not yet tell why the society wanted it.

But I had been asked to find out.

This was the first task of my working relationship.

I would see it through.

---

I did not go directly to Silas Wren.

I had learned, in two years of keeping, that the direct approach was the approach that failed. I went to the market first, and I asked, where I could ask without drawing attention, about a collector who kept objects without history.

The market knew of him. The town knew of him, in the way a town knows of a man who keeps to himself and collects strange things.

"He is not from the town," said a woman at the cloth stall. "He came, years ago. He bought the old house on the lane past the mill. He has not left it since, that anyone has seen."

"He is a collector," said a man at the tool stall. "He buys things â€” old things, strange things â€” and he does not tell anyone where they come from. He has a house full of them."

"What does he collect?" I asked.

"Things with no history," the man said. "That is what they say. Coins with no year. Books with no author. He is the man that buys the things that have been scrubbed of their origin."

I thanked them. I went home.

Silas Wren had a house on the lane past the mill. He collected objects without history. He had not left the house in years, that anyone had seen.

And he had a book that the society wanted.

I did not know how I would get to see it.

But I had learned, in two years of keeping, that a man who keeps a house and does not leave it is a man who can be watched â€” and that a man who can be watched can be understood.

---

I watched the house for a week.

It stood on the lane past the mill, set back from the road, behind a wall of old stone. The windows were dark. No one came in or out that I saw, and no one left, and the house sat in its silence the way a house sits when it has been kept for a long time.

I did not approach it. I watched from the lane, and from the fields beyond, and from the mill-yard corner, where I had a reason to be, and I learned the house's habits.

There were none.

The house gave no sign of life. No lamp in the evening. No smoke from the chimney. No movement at the windows. It was as if the house were empty â€” and yet I knew it was not, because the society had told me Silas Wren kept it, and because the town said he had not left it in years.

On the seventh day, I saw him.

I was at the mill-yard corner, at dusk, and a light appeared in the upper window of the house â€” a small light, the kind of light a man lights when he is reading. It burned for an hour, and then it was carried through the house, from room to room, the way the lamp in the end house was carried, and then it went out.

I watched the light go out, and I understood something.

Silas Wren was in the house. He read at night. He carried his light through the rooms, the way a keeper carries a lamp, and then he put it out.

He was a keeper of sorts.

A keeper of objects that had been scrubbed of their history.

And he had a book that the society wanted.

I went home, and I wrote it in the red book, and I understood that I had been given a task that would require more than watching.

It would require entering.

---

I went to see Ysabel.

She was at her house near the river, and she let me in, and I told her about the task.

"Silas Wren," she said slowly. "The collector of objects without history."

"You know him."

"I have heard of him." She looked at me. "He is not easy to approach. He does not receive visitors. He does not sell. He does not show."

"The society wants a book he has," I said.

"And they have asked you to find out what is in it."

"Yes."

Ysabel was silent a moment.

"I will tell you what I know," she said. "Silas Wren believes that the objects in his collection have one thing in common: that someone deliberately erased their origin. He believes they were scrubbed â€” that their histories were removed, on purpose, so that they could not be traced."

"Why would anyone do that?"

"That is what he is trying to find out," Ysabel said. "He has spent years collecting the objects, and years trying to understand why their origins were erased. He believes there is a pattern."

"A pattern of what?"

"I do not know." She looked at me. "But if there is a book in his collection that the society wants, it may be the book that explains the pattern."

I stood in her house, in the cold light, and I understood.

The book was not just a book. It was a key â€” a key to the pattern that Silas Wren had spent years trying to understand.

And the society wanted it.

I did not know what I would do.

But I knew that I would enter the house.

I would find the book.

And I would learn what was in it.

---

I went to the house on the eighth night.

I did not approach by the lane. I came across the fields, in the dark, the way a man comes when he does not want to be seen, and I came to the wall of old stone, and I found the gate, and the gate was not locked.

I stood at the gate a long moment.

I did not know whether I was doing the right thing. I had been asked to find out what was in the book, not to steal it. But I did not know how to find out what was in a book without opening it.

I pushed the gate open. I crossed the garden. I came to the door of the house.

It was not locked.

I pushed it open.

The house was dark, and cold, and filled with objects.

I stood in the doorway, in the dark, and I looked at the room â€” and I understood that I had entered the collection of Silas Wren.

Coins without years. Books without authors. Paintings without signatures. Clocks without mechanisms. Photographs without faces. Everywhere, objects that had been scrubbed of their origin.

And at the back of the room, on a table, under a lamp that was not lit, there was a book.

I crossed the room. I reached for the book.

And a voice spoke from the dark.

"I wondered when you would come," said Silas Wren.

I turned.

He stood in the shadow of the stair, in a dark coat, with his hands at his sides. He was neither old nor young. His eyes were the eyes of a man who had waited a long time for a door to be opened.

"You are the keeper," he said. "You are the man who found the clock, and the key, and the lock, and the letter of Aurelius Ashcroft."

I did not deny it.

"And you have come for my book," he said.

I was silent.

He looked at me a long moment. Then he smiled â€” a small smile, tired and knowing.

"Then you are the man I have been waiting for," he said. "Sit down. I will show you what is in it."

I stood in the dark room, among the objects without history, and I understood that I had not broken into Silas Wren's house.

I had walked into a room that had been waiting for me.

And the book was the reason.

CHAPTER 107 â€” THE BOOK OF ORIGINS

Silas Wren lit the lamp.

It was the same lamp I had seen from the mill-yard corner â€” a small lamp, old, with a glass that had been mended once. He set it on the table, beside the book, and the light fell on the pages, and I saw what the book was.

It was a ledger, bound in black leather, its spine cracked with age. The pages were filled with a hand I had seen before â€” in the margins of the vestry book, in the pages of the wardens' ledger, in the letter of Aurelius Ashcroft.

The same hand.

I looked at Silas.

"You know this hand," he said.

"I know it."

"It is the hand of the man who erased the origins," he said. "The man who collected these objects, and who removed their histories, and who left them in this town for others to find."

I looked at the book. The hand of the man who had erased the origins.

"It is the hand of Aurelius Ashcroft," I said.

Silas was silent a moment.

"No," he said. "It is the hand of the man who came before him."

I stood very still.

"The man who came before Aurelius Ashcroft," I said.

"The keeper before the keeper," Silas said. "The man who built the first sign, and who carved it into the stone, and who set the boundary, and who made the first agreement. The man whose name is not in any record."

I looked at the book. The hand of the man who had come before Aurelius.

"His name is not known," Silas said. "He was the first keeper. He built Bellweather House. He set the boundary. He carved the first sign. And then he erased his own history â€” not because he was afraid, but because he wanted the keeping to be about the thing, and not about the man."

I stood in the lamplight, among the objects without history, and I understood.

The objects in Silas Wren's collection had not been scrubbed by someone else. They had been scrubbed by the first keeper â€” the man who had come before Aurelius, who had built the first sign, and who had erased his own history so that the keeping would be about the thing, and not about the man.

"This book," Silas said, "is the record of what he erased. It is the account of the origins â€” the origins of the objects, the origins of the signs, the origins of the keeping. It is the only record that remains."

"And you have been keeping it," I said.

He was silent a moment.

"I am not a member of the society," he said. "I am a collector. I collect the things that have been erased. And I have been holding this book until the keeper came."

I stood in the room, with the book before me, and I understood.

The society had not sent me to steal the book. They had sent me to find it â€” to find the man who was holding it, and to receive it from him.

Because they had known that Silas Wren would not sell it, and would not show it, and would not give it to anyone â€” except the keeper.

I looked at Silas.

"You have been holding it for me," I said.

"For you," he said. "Take it. It is yours."

I reached out. I took the book.

It was heavy in my hands. The leather was cracked, the pages yellow, the ink brown with age. The hand of the first keeper was on every page.

I did not open it then.

I stood in the lamplight, with the book in my hands, and I understood that I had been given something that had been waiting for me for a very long time.

The account of the origins.

The record of what the first keeper had erased.

The book of the first sign.

The account of the origins.

The hand of the first keeper.

The man who had set the boundary, and carved the first sign, and built Bellweather House, and erased his own history so that the keeping would be about the thing, and not about the man.

I sat in the dark, with the book before me, and I understood that I had been given something that I was not ready to read.

The account of the origins was not a book of answers. It was a book of beginnings â€” the beginning of the keeping, the beginning of the signs, the beginning of the boundary.

And I was not ready to read it.

I was still learning what the keeping was.

I would open the book when I was ready.

I put it in the drawer, beside the red book, and the agreement, and the key to the reading room.

I sat in the dark.

I was the keeper. I was a hunter. I was a man who worked with a society that had no name.

And I had been given the book of the first keeper.

I could not yet tell what was in it.

But I knew that I would open it, when the time was right.

The hunt had led me to the book.

And the book was the beginning of something new.

CHAPTER 108 â€” THE OPENING

I kept the book of the first keeper for three days without opening it.

I could not yet tell why I waited. I had been given the book, and the book was the account of the origins, and the account was what I had been seeking â€” the beginning of the keeping, the beginning of the signs, the beginning of the boundary.

But I did not open it.

I set it on the desk each evening. I looked at it. I did not open it.

The red book lay beside it â€” the account I had written, the account of my own keeping. And the book of the first keeper lay beside the red book, and I looked at them together, and I understood that one was the beginning and one was the keeping, and I did not know whether I was ready to read the beginning.

On the third evening, I opened it.

---

The first page was not a page of words. It was a page of marks.

Not the first sign. Not the keeper's sign. Not the circle and the line. A page of marks I had never seen â€” small marks, like a tally, running in rows across the page, hundreds of them, in the hand of the first keeper.

I looked at them for a long time.

I did not know what they meant. I did not know whether they were a count, or a record, or a language, or a pattern.

I turned the page.

The second page was words â€” the hand of the first keeper, in the same careful hand I had seen in the letter of Aurelius, but older, harder, written by a hand that had not yet learned the gentleness of the later hand.

I read it.

*I have set the boundary. I have carved the sign. I have built the house. I have made the agreement.*

*I have done these things because I was asked to do them, and because I believed they were necessary, and because I did not know what else to do.*

*I do not know what lies beneath the boundary. I do not know what the sign marks. I do not know what the house keeps.*

*I know only that I was asked, and that I said yes, and that I have kept my word.*

I turned the page.

*The tally is the count of the years. I have kept it since the first year, one mark for each year, so that the keeper who comes after will know how long the keeping has stood.*

*I do not know how many years the keeping will stand. I do not know who will keep it after me. I do not know whether the keeper who comes after will understand what I have done.*

*But I know that I have kept my word, and that the keeping is the thing, and that the man is not.*

I read it twice.

*The keeping is the thing, and the man is not.*

I had written the same words, in my own way, in the red book. I had not known that the first keeper had written them too.

I turned the pages.

The book was not long. It was the account of a life â€” the life of the first keeper, who had set the boundary, and carved the sign, and built the house, and made the agreement, and kept the tally, and erased his own history so that the keeping would be about the thing, and not about the man.

There was no name in the book. There was no record of where he came from, or who he was, or what he had been before he became the keeper.

Only the account of the keeping.

And at the end of the book, on the last page, there was a single line, in a hand that had grown tired with years:

*I have kept my word. I have kept the keeping. I have erased myself so that the keeping will be kept for its own sake, and not for mine. Let the keeper who comes after know that the keeping is the thing, and the man is not.*

I closed the book.

I sat in the dark, with the book before me, and I understood.

The first keeper had not been a man of power. He had been a man who had been asked to keep something, and who had said yes, and who had kept it for his whole life, and who had erased himself so that the keeping would be kept for its own sake.

The boundary. The sign. The house. The agreement. The tally.

All of it, kept by a man whose name was not in any record.

And I had been given the account of what he had done.

I sat in the dark, and I understood that I was not reading the beginning of a mystery.

I was reading the beginning of a life.

A life that had been given to the keeping.

And I understood, sitting in the dark, that I had been given the same life.

---

I did not tell the society what was in the book.

I did not tell Adeline. I did not tell the old man. I did not tell Ysabel.

I sat at my desk, with the book of the first keeper before me, and I understood that I had been given something that was not for the society.

The society wanted the book. The society had asked me to find out what was in it. But the book was not a thing to be given to a society.

The book was the account of the first keeper's life.

And the account was the keeping.

I put the book in the drawer, beside the red book, and the agreement, and the key to the reading room.

I did not know what I would tell the society.

I did not know whether I would tell them anything.

But I knew that the book was not mine to give, and not theirs to take.

The book was the account of the keeping.

And the account was the thing that was kept.

---

I went to the reading room the following evening.

Adeline was there, at the table, with the lamp burning.

"Did you find the book?" she said.

"I found it."

"And what is in it?"

I was silent a moment.

"It is the account of the first keeper," I said. "The man who set the boundary, and carved the sign, and built the house, and made the agreement. He kept a tally of the years, and he wrote the account of the keeping, and he erased his own history so that the keeping would be kept for its own sake."

Adeline was silent.

"And the book?" she said.

"I have it," I said. "I am keeping it."

She looked at me.

"The society asked you to find out what was in it," she said. "Not to keep it."

"The society asked me to find out what was in it," I agreed. "And I have found out. It is the account of the first keeper's life. And I am keeping it, because it is the account of the keeping, and the account is the thing that is kept."

Adeline was silent a long moment.

"You have changed," she said. "Since you came to the room above the chandler's shop."

"I have understood," I said.

"Understood what?"

"That the society does not own what it finds," I said. "That a keeper keeps what is given to him, and does not give it to another. And that the book of the first keeper was not given to me to pass on â€” it was given to me to keep."

I looked at her.

"I will work with you," I said. "I will help you preserve what would otherwise be lost. But I will not give you the book."

Adeline looked at me for a long time.

Then she nodded.

"That," she said, "is the answer I hoped you would give."

She stood.

"Keep the book," she said. "It is yours. It was always meant to be yours."

She went to the stair.

"The society does not need the book," she said. "The society needed to know that the man who found it would keep it â€” and would not give it away, to the society or to anyone."

She went down the stair.

I stood in the reading room, in the lamplight, with the key in my pocket and the book in my drawer, and I understood that the society had been testing me all along.

Not the room. Not the box. Not the name.

The book.

They had wanted to know whether the keeper who found the book would keep it.

And I had kept it.

I walked home through the dark streets, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*I have read the book of the first keeper. It is the account of his life. He set the boundary, and carved the sign, and built the house, and made the agreement, and kept the tally, and erased himself so that the keeping would be kept for its own sake.*

*The keeping is the thing, and the man is not.*

*I have kept the book. I will not give it to the society. It was given to me to keep, and I have kept it.*

*The society has tested me for the last time.*

*And I have kept the thing that was given to me to keep.*



===== FILE: Chapter_109_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 109 â€” THE TALLY

I did not see Adeline Brume for two weeks after the night in the reading room.

I did not expect to. I had kept the book. I had refused to give it to the society. And I had understood that the society had been testing me â€” that the book had been the final test, and that I had passed by keeping it.

I could not yet tell what the society would do next. I could not yet tell whether they would ask more of me, or whether they would leave me be, or whether the working relationship had ended before it had truly begun.

I kept the account. I walked the boundary. I carried the medallions and the gear. I wrote in the red book.

And I read the book of the first keeper.

I read it slowly, a page at a time, in the evenings, and I did not rush it. The book was not long, but it was the account of a life, and I read it as a life should be read â€” carefully, without hurry.

The first keeper had set the boundary. He had carved the sign. He had built the house. He had made the agreement. He had kept the tally.

And at the end of the book, on the last page, he had written the sentence I had already read: *I have kept my word. I have kept the keeping. I have erased myself so that the keeping will be kept for its own sake, and not for mine.*

I read the sentence many times.

And I began to count the tally.

---

The tally was the page of marks I had seen on the first page â€” small marks, like tally-marks, running in rows across the page.

I had looked at them once, and I had not understood them. I had turned the page, and I had read the words, and I had not gone back to the marks.

Now I went back.

I took the book, and I opened it to the first page, and I looked at the tally, and I counted.

The marks ran in rows. Each row had a certain number of marks. The rows were arranged in groups.

I counted the marks.

There were more than I had expected. Many more than the years of the keeping as I knew it â€” more than the fifty years of Vale, more than the twenty years of the keeper before him, more than the years of Aurelius.

I counted again.

The tally was not a count of the years of the keeping as I knew it.

It was a count of something older.

I looked at the marks, and I understood that I did not know how to read them. I did not know what the first keeper was counting. I did not know whether the marks were years, or months, or something else entirely.

I set the book down.

I sat in the dark, and I thought about the tally.

The first keeper had written that the tally was the count of the years. He had written: "I have kept it since the first year, one mark for each year, so that the keeper who comes after will know how long the keeping has stood."

And I had counted the marks, and the marks were more than the years of the keeping as I knew it.

I did not know what that meant.

I did not know whether the tally was longer than the keeping I knew, or whether the keeping was older than I had believed, or whether I had counted wrong.

I opened the book again. I counted the marks again.

The count was the same.

I sat in the dark, with the book before me, and I understood that the tally was not a thing I could read â€” not yet.

I had read the words. I had understood the account of the first keeper's life.

But the tally â€” the count of the years â€” was a language I did not yet speak.

I put the book in the drawer, beside the red book.

I did not know what the tally counted.

But I knew that I would learn.

---

I went to see Ysabel the following day.

She was at her house near the river, and she let me in, and I set the book on her table.

"The book of the first keeper," I said. "I have read it. It is the account of his life â€” the boundary, the sign, the house, the agreement, the tally."

"The tally," Ysabel said.

"There is a page of marks at the beginning," I said. "A tally. He writes that it is the count of the years â€” one mark for each year, kept since the first year."

"And you have counted them."

"I have counted them," I said. "And the count is more than the years of the keeping as I know it. More than Vale's fifty years, more than the keeper before him, more than Aurelius."

Ysabel was silent a moment.

"More than the years of the keeping as you know it," she repeated.

"Yes."

"Then either the tally is not a count of years," she said, "or the keeping is older than you have believed, or the tally is counting something else."

I looked at her.

"That is what I thought," I said. "I do not know which."

She looked at the book. She did not open it.

"The first keeper wrote that the keeping is the thing, and the man is not," she said. "If that is true, then the tally is not the count of one man's years. It is the count of the keeping's years â€” the years the keeping has stood, whoever kept it."

I stood in her house, in the cold light, and I understood.

The tally was not the count of the first keeper's life.

It was the count of the keeping itself â€” the years the keeping had stood, from the first keeper to the present, from the setting of the boundary to the keeping of the account.

And the count was more than I had believed.

Because the keeping was older than the keepers I knew.

I did not know how old.

But I knew that I would find out.

---

I did not open the book again that week.

I walked the boundary. I stood at the eastern marker. I looked at the first sign, carved into the stone, worn smooth by years.

The first sign. The mark of the maker. The mark that was on the medallion, and on the clocks, and on the box, and on the bell, and on the door.

I had carried the medallion all my life. I had found the clocks, and the key, and the lock. I had read the letter of Aurelius. I had read the book of the first keeper.

And now I had found the tally â€” the count of the keeping's years.

I did not know what the count was.

But I knew that it was longer than the keeping I knew.

And I knew that the keeping was older than the keepers I knew.

I stood at the eastern marker, in the grey light, and I understood that the mystery had not begun with Aurelius, and had not begun with Vale, and had not begun with the first keeper.

It had begun before them â€” before the boundary, before the sign, before the house, before the agreement.

And the tally was the count of those years.

I walked home through the fields, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*I have read the book of the first keeper. He kept a tally â€” one mark for each year, kept since the first year.*

*I have counted the marks. The count is more than the years of the keeping as I know it.*

*The tally is not the count of one man's years. It is the count of the keeping's years â€” the years the keeping has stood, whoever kept it.*

*The keeping is older than the keepers I know.*

*I do not know how old.*

*But I will find out.*

CHAPTER 110 â€” THE OLDER KEEPING

I spent the autumn trying to understand the tally.

I read the book of the first keeper again, and again, and again. I copied the page of marks into my notebook. I counted them by ones, by fives, by rows. I counted them forward and backward. The count was always the same.

The tally was longer than the keeping as I knew it.

I could not yet tell how much longer. I could not yet tell whether the tally was a count of years, or of seasons, or of something else. I could not yet tell whether I had counted the marks correctly, or whether the marks meant something other than what the first keeper had said they meant.

But I knew that the keeping was older than the keepers I knew.

And I knew that I had to find out how old.

---

I went to the vestry.

The clerk was there, as he always was, at his table, with the books and the keys. He looked up when I came in.

"You have come about something," he said.

"I have come about the records," I said. "The oldest records you have. The records of the boundary, and the sign, and the house."

The clerk looked at me a long moment.

"The oldest records," he said slowly. "The survey records the wall. The book of the wall records the stones. The vestry book records the parish. But the oldest records â€” the records that came before the survey, before the book of the wall, before the vestry â€” those are not in the vestry."

"Where are they?"

The clerk was silent.

"There is a record," he said, "that is older than the vestry. It is kept in the Academy, in the room at the top of the stair, where the papers are kept that no one is shown unless they ask for the oldest things."

"The Academy copy," I said.

"The Academy keeps copies," the clerk said. "It keeps the schedule of the year four, and the letter of T. Wren, and the records of the trust. But the oldest thing it keeps is older than all of those."

I waited.

"There is a book," the clerk said, "in the Academy's keeping, that has no date and no author. It is the record of the boundary as it was first set. It is older than the survey, and older than the book of the wall, and older than the vestry book."

I stood in the vestry.

A book older than the survey, and the book of the wall, and the vestry book. The record of the boundary as it was first set.

"Who has seen it?" I said.

"Very few," the clerk said. "I have seen it once, in my father's time. It is kept in the Academy, and it is not shown, because it is the oldest thing the Academy keeps, and the Academy keeps the oldest things the way a man keeps the oldest things â€” without showing them."

I thanked the clerk. I left the vestry.

The Academy had a book older than the survey, and the book of the wall, and the vestry book. The record of the boundary as it was first set.

And I had the tally â€” the count of the keeping's years.

I did not know whether the book and the tally were connected.

But I knew that I had to see the book.

---

I went to the Academy in the afternoon.

Daniel was there, in the room at the top of the stair, with his papers and his ledgers, and he looked up when I came in.

"You have come about the oldest things," he said.

"You know why I have come."

"I have heard," Daniel said. "The clerk has spoken of it. There is a book in the Academy's keeping that has no date and no author â€” the record of the boundary as it was first set."

"And you will show it to me?"

Daniel was silent a moment.

"I have never shown it to anyone," he said. "I have looked at it, once. It is the oldest thing the Academy keeps."

"And you will show it to me?"

He looked at me a long moment.

"You have asked for the oldest things," he said. "And you have kept what you have been given. I will show it to you."

He went to the shelf at the back of the room, where the oldest books were kept, and he took down a book â€” a book bound in leather so old that it had gone the colour of the dust, its spine held together with thread.

He set it on the table.

"The record of the boundary," he said. "As it was first set."

I opened it.

The first page was not a page of words. It was a page of marks.

The same marks as the tally.

Rows of marks, in the same hand, running across the page.

I stood very still.

The tally in the book of the first keeper.

And the same marks, in the same hand, in the oldest book in the Academy.

"The marks," Daniel said. "You know them."

"I know them," I said. "There is a page of the same marks in the book of the first keeper. He wrote that they are the count of the years â€” one mark for each year, kept since the first year."

"And this book," Daniel said, "records the boundary as it was first set. If the marks are the same, in the same hand..."

I did not let him finish.

I counted the marks in the Academy book.

The count was the same as the tally.

The same number of marks. The same rows. The same groups.

Two books. Two pages of the same marks. The same hand.

I stood in the room at the top of the stair, and I understood.

The tally in the book of the first keeper was not a count of the keeping's years.

It was a count that had been made before the boundary was set.

The same hand that had set the boundary had made the tally.

And the count was older than the boundary itself.

---

I did not tell Daniel what I had concluded.

I thanked him. I left the Academy. I walked home through the streets of Ashford, with the two counts in my mind â€” the tally in the book of the first keeper, and the marks in the oldest book of the Academy, the same marks, in the same hand, the same count.

The boundary had been set by the first keeper.

The tally had been made by the first keeper.

And the marks in the oldest book of the Academy were the same marks, in the same hand.

The count was older than the boundary.

I did not know what that meant. I did not know what the count was counting. I did not know why the first keeper had made the same tally in two books.

But I knew that the keeping was older than the boundary.

And I knew that the boundary had been set to mark something that had been counted before.

I sat at my desk that evening, with the two counts before me, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*The tally in the book of the first keeper is the same as the marks in the oldest book of the Academy. The same hand. The same count.*

*The boundary was set by the first keeper. The tally was made by the first keeper. And the same tally stands in the oldest record of the boundary.*

*The count is older than the boundary.*

*I do not know what the count is counting.*

*But I know that it was counted before the boundary was set.*

*And I know that the boundary was set to mark something that had been counted before.*

I closed the red book.

I sat in the dark.

I was the keeper. I was a hunter. I was a man who worked with a society that had no name.

And I had found a count that was older than the boundary.

I did not know what it was counting.

But I knew that I would find out.

The hunt had led me to the tally.

And the tally was leading me somewhere older.

CHAPTER 111 â€” THE COUNT

I could not yet tell what the count was counting.

I carried the two pages of marks with me, in my notebook â€” the tally from the book of the first keeper, and the marks from the oldest book of the Academy. I looked at them together, and I counted them, and I did not know what they meant.

The count was a number. I had counted it many times. It was the same number each time.

But I did not know what it was counting.

I sat at my desk, with the two pages before me, and I thought about the keeping â€” the boundary, the sign, the house, the agreement, the door, the bell. I thought about the keepers â€” the first keeper, Aurelius, the keeper before Vale, Vale, and me. I thought about the years â€” the fifty years of Vale, the years of the keeper before him, the years of Aurelius.

The count was more than all of them.

I did not know what the count was counting.

---

I went to see Ysabel.

She was at her house near the river, and I set my notebook on her table, and I showed her the two pages of marks.

"The tally from the book of the first keeper," I said. "And the marks from the oldest book of the Academy. The same hand. The same count."

Ysabel looked at them for a long time.

"The first keeper wrote that the tally is the count of the years," she said. "One mark for each year, kept since the first year."

"Yes."

"And the oldest book of the Academy records the boundary as it was first set."

"Yes."

"And the marks are the same, in the same hand, the same count."

"Yes."

Ysabel was silent a moment.

"Then either the first keeper made the tally twice," she said, "or the boundary was not the first thing he set."

I looked at her.

"The boundary was not the first thing he set?"

"The count was made before the boundary," Ysabel said. "You have said so yourself. The same hand made the same count in two books â€” one the tally of the keeping, one the record of the boundary. If the count is the same, and the hand is the same, then the count was made at the same time in both books."

"And?"

"And the count was made before the boundary," she said. "Or the count was made for the boundary."

I stood in her house, in the cold light, and I did not understand.

"The count was made for the boundary," I said.

"The count is a number," Ysabel said. "A number of things. The first keeper made the count, and then he set the boundary. Perhaps the boundary was set to mark the count."

"To mark the count?"

"Perhaps," she said, "the boundary does not mark a place. Perhaps it marks a number."

I looked at her.

"A number of what?"

"I do not know," she said. "But if the boundary marks a number, then the stones are not markers of a boundary â€” they are counters. And the tally is not a count of years â€” it is the record of what the boundary is counting."

I stood in her house, and I understood that I had been reading the boundary wrong.

I had read it as a line â€” a boundary drawn between one place and another. I had read the stones as markers of that line.

But if the boundary marked a number, then the stones were counters â€” and the line was not a line at all.

The line was a count.

---

I went to the boundary the following morning.

I walked the line of stones, from the eastern marker to the corner where the wall turned, and I counted the stones.

I had counted them before. There were more than a hundred stones along the wall.

I counted them again.

The number was not the same as the tally.

I stood at the fourth stone, where the hairline mark ran across its face, and I looked at the stone, and I thought about the tally.

The tally was a number. The stones were a number. The two numbers were not the same.

I did not know what that meant.

I knelt at the fourth stone. I looked at the first sign, cut into the base of the stone, hidden where the earth covered it.

The first sign. The mark of the maker.

The tally was in the hand of the first keeper.

The stones were set by the first keeper.

The boundary was set to mark something that had been counted before.

I sat back on my heels, and I looked at the stone, and I understood that I was reading the boundary wrong.

I had read it as a line. I had read the stones as markers of the line.

But the stones were not markers of a line. They were counters. Each stone counted something â€” one thing for each stone, or some number of things for each stone.

And the tally was the record of what they counted.

I stood.

I walked the line of stones again, and I counted them again, and I looked at each stone as I passed, and I looked for the pattern â€” the pattern of the count, the pattern that the boundary was marking.

I did not find it that morning.

But I understood that the boundary was not a line.

It was a count.

And the tally was the record of the count.

---

I went home.

I sat at my desk. I opened the red book. I wrote:

*The boundary is not a line. It is a count.*

*The stones are not markers of a line. They are counters. Each stone counts something.*

*The tally in the book of the first keeper is the record of the count. The same count stands in the oldest book of the Academy, beside the record of the boundary.*

*I do not know what the stones are counting.*

*But I know that the boundary was set to mark the count.*

*And I know that the count is older than the boundary.*

*I will learn what the stones are counting.*

*I will learn the count.*

I closed the red book.

I sat in the dark.

I was the keeper. I was a hunter. I was a man who worked with a society that had no name.

And I had found the count.

I did not know what it was counting.

But I knew that I would learn.

The hunt had led me to the boundary.

And the boundary was leading me to the count.

CHAPTER 112 â€” THE WALL OF STONES

I returned to the boundary the following day, and the day after, and the day after that.

I walked the line of stones from the eastern marker to the corner where the wall turned, and I looked at each stone, and I counted them, and I looked for the pattern â€” the pattern of the count, the pattern that the stones were marking.

I counted the stones. More than a hundred. I counted them again. The number was the same.

But the number was not the tally.

I knelt at the first stone. I looked at the first sign, cut into the base of the stone, hidden where the earth covered it. The first sign. The mark of the maker.

I moved to the second stone. The same sign. The same mark, cut into the base, hidden.

The third. The fourth. The fifth.

Every stone had the first sign at its base.

Every stone carried the mark of the maker.

I walked the length of the wall, and I looked at every stone, and every stone had the first sign at its base.

The tally was a number. The stones were a number. Every stone had the first sign.

But the number of stones was not the tally.

I stood at the end of the wall, and I could not yet tell what the stones were counting.

---

I went to the vestry.

The clerk was there.

"The wall," I said. "The stones. Every stone has the first sign at its base."

"Yes."

"Every stone is marked."

"Yes."

I stood in the vestry.

"Then the stones are not the count," I said. "The stones are the counters. Each stone is a counter, and the tally is the record of what they count."

The clerk was silent.

"The tally is a number," he said. "The stones are a number. The number of stones is not the tally. So the stones are not counting themselves."

"Then what are they counting?"

The clerk was silent a long moment.

"I do not know," he said. "I have kept the books for forty years, and I have not known what the stones are counting."

I stood in the vestry.

The stones were counters. The tally was the record. And no one knew what they were counting.

---

I went back to the boundary.

I walked the line of stones again, and I did not count them. I looked at them â€” looked at the stones themselves, not the number of them.

The stones were old. They were worn by weather, by years, by the passing of hands that no longer remembered whose.

Every stone had the first sign at its base.

Every stone carried the mark of the maker.

I knelt at the fourth stone, where the hairline mark ran across its face.

The first sign at the base.

The hairline mark across the face.

Two marks on the same stone.

I looked at the hairline mark. It was a crack â€” or a mark, I did not know which. It ran across the stone, straight, too straight to be natural.

I had watched it appear, in the spring of the first year. It had appeared, and it had not grown, and I had not known what it meant.

I looked at the first sign at the base of the stone.

The first sign. The mark of the maker.

The hairline mark. The mark that had appeared, and had not grown, and had not been explained.

Two marks on the same stone.

I did not know what they meant.

But I knew that the stones were counting something.

And I knew that the marks on the stones were the language of the count.

I stood.

I walked home.

I sat at my desk.

I opened the red book. I wrote:

*Every stone has the first sign at its base. Every stone is a counter. The tally is the record of what they count.*

*I do not know what the stones are counting.*

*But I know that the stones are counting something.*

*I will learn what it is.*

*The hunt has led me to the wall.*

*And the wall is leading me to the count.*

CHAPTER 113 â€” THE MEDALLION AND THE COUNT

I had not worn the medallion in months.

It lay in the drawer, with the gear, and the red book, and the agreement, and the key to the reading room. I had not worn it since the winter, when I had carried it to the chamber beneath Bellweather House and set it in the first sign on the floor.

I took it out now.

It lay in my hand, cold, the first sign on its face â€” the circle, the three lines, the dot. The mark of the maker. The same mark that was on every stone of the wall, at the base, hidden where the earth covered it.

I held it up, and I looked at it, and I thought about the count.

The stones had the first sign at their base. The medallion had the first sign on its face. The tally was in the hand of the first keeper. The boundary was set to mark the count.

I could not yet tell what the stones were counting.

But I knew that the first sign was the language of the count.

And the medallion was the first sign.

I put the cord around my neck. I wore the medallion against my chest, the way I had worn it in the years before, when I had carried it without knowing what it was.

I wore it to the boundary.

---

I walked the line of stones, with the medallion warm against my chest.

The medallion had always warmed near the keeper's sign, near the door, near the stones. I had tested it, in the first year, and it had warmed at the keeper's sign in the earth, and at the first sign on the wall of the house, and at the door.

I walked the boundary now, and I watched the medallion.

It warmed at the first stone.

It warmed at the second.

It warmed at the third, and the fourth, and the fifth.

It warmed at every stone.

I stood at the end of the wall, and I looked at the medallion, and I understood.

The medallion warmed at every stone â€” because every stone carried the first sign.

The first sign on the medallion was the same as the first sign on the stones.

And the medallion was warm.

I did not know what that meant.

But I knew that the medallion was connected to the stones â€” to every stone, not just to the boundary as a whole.

The medallion was the first sign.

The stones were the first sign.

The count was the record of the first sign.

I stood at the end of the wall, with the warm medallion against my chest, and I understood that I had been carrying the key to the count all my life.

The medallion.

The first sign.

I had carried it without knowing what it was.

Now I was beginning to understand.

---

I went to the chamber beneath Bellweather House.

I had not been there since the winter. I had not needed to go. The chamber was there, and the first sign was on the floor, and I had set the medallion in it once, and I had felt the place beneath.

I went now.

I entered the house at the end of the lane. I crossed the bare room. I knelt at the trapdoor and opened it.

I went down into the passage.

The first signs marked the way â€” the circle, the three lines, the dot, carved into the stone at intervals.

I walked the passage, with the medallion warm against my chest, and I came to the chamber.

The first sign was on the floor.

I knelt.

I took out the medallion. I set it in the first sign on the floor, the way I had set it in the winter.

The stone warmed.

I put my hand to the floor, beside the medallion. The warmth rose through the stone, into my hand.

The place beneath.

I knelt there, with the medallion set in the first sign, and I did not take it away.

I stayed.

I did not know how long I stayed.

And then I understood.

The first sign on the floor of the chamber was the same as the first sign on every stone of the wall.

The chamber was not separate from the boundary. The chamber was part of it.

The first sign on the floor was the centre of the count.

And the medallion, set in the first sign, was the counter.

I did not know what the count was counting.

But I understood that I was part of it.

The keeper, and the medallion, and the first sign, and the stones.

All of it, one count.

---

I took the medallion from the floor.

I stood.

I left the chamber. I walked back along the passage, past the first signs, up the ladder, and out of the house.

I walked home through the streets, with the medallion warm against my chest, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*The medallion warms at every stone. The stones carry the first sign at their base. The chamber carries the first sign on its floor. The medallion is the first sign.*

*The boundary is a count. The stones are counters. The chamber is the centre of the count. The medallion is the counter.*

*I do not know what the count is counting.*

*But I know that I am part of it.*

*The keeper, and the medallion, and the first sign, and the stones.*

*All of it, one count.*

I closed the red book.

I sat in the dark.

I was the keeper. I was a hunter.

And I was part of the count.

I did not know what it was counting.

But I would learn.

The hunt had led me to the medallion.

And the medallion was leading me to the count.

CHAPTER 114 â€” THE SEASON RETURNS

The season came again to Ashford.

I had known it would come. I had known it for years, since the first winter, when I had stood at the end of the lane and watched the lamp burn at nine and heard the bell at the corner. The season came every year, from the thirteenth to the twenty-first of November, and it ceased on the twenty-second.

I had kept the account through four seasons now.

I had watched the lamp burn, and the bell come, and the house at the end of the lane keep itself through the season. I had walked the boundary, and the wall, and the passage. I had kept the door closed, and the bell silent, and the houses kept.

The season came again.

And this year, I understood it differently.

---

I stood at the end of the lane on the thirteenth of November.

The light was failing. The town was settling into its evening. The lamps were being lit along the High Street, and the windows were taking light one by one.

The end house stood in its place, dark, with the rosemary in its pot at the sill.

I waited.

At nine, the lamp came.

It burned in the upper window, steady, at its old height. And then it was carried away along the rooms, the way it had been carried every year, without a servant's voice, without footsteps.

I watched it.

I had watched it for years. I had noted it. I had written it in the red book.

I had not understood it.

Now I understood it differently.

The lamp was not a ghost. It was not a haunting. It was a keeping â€” a keeping that had stood for longer than the town had a name.

And the keeping was part of the count.

I could not yet tell what the count was counting. But I knew that the lamp was part of it.

The lamp burned at nine. The bell came when the lamp did not. The house kept itself through the season.

All of it, part of the count.

I stood at the end of the lane, with the medallion warm against my chest, and I understood that I was watching the count.

---

I went to the common on the night of the fifteenth.

The house was there.

It stood at the edge of the common, where the grass met the lower pasture, its windows dark, its front door open.

I had entered it once, in the first year. I had found the clock stopped at 11:17. I had heard the three knocks from beneath the floor. I had not entered it again.

I stood at the edge of the common now, in the dark, and I looked at the house.

It had appeared, and disappeared, for years. It had left only the flattened grass, and the keeper's sign pressed into the earth.

I did not approach it.

I stood at the edge of the common, with the medallion warm against my chest, and I looked at the house.

The medallion was warm.

I had not felt it warm at the common for a long time â€” not since the first year, when I had carried the medallion to the keeper's sign in the earth, and it had warmed, and I had understood that the first sign responded to the keeper's sign.

It warmed now.

It warmed at the edge of the common, where the house appeared.

I stood in the dark, and I looked at the house, and I understood that the house was part of the count.

The house appeared. The house disappeared. The house kept something â€” the first sign, carved into its wall, hidden where only the medallion could find it.

The house was part of the count.

I did not enter it.

I stood at the edge of the common, and I watched the house, and I waited.

---

I went to the tower on the night of the twentieth.

I had the key. I had always had the key â€” the keeper's key, to the tower, to the bell.

I climbed the stairs. I stood in the bell chamber.

The bell hung in the dark, silent, the first sign on its rim.

I had not rung it. I had not needed to ring it. The bell was the keeper's bell, and it was kept, and it was not rung.

I stood in the bell chamber, in the dark, and I looked at the bell.

The first sign was on its rim.

The bell was part of the count.

I did not ring it.

I stood in the bell chamber, and I understood that I was watching the count.

---

The season ended on the twenty-second of November.

The lamp came, and the bell came, and the house kept itself through the season. And on the twenty-second, it ceased.

I stood at the end of the lane on the morning of the twenty-second, and I looked at the end house, and I understood that the season had passed.

The count had turned.

I did not know what the count was counting. I did not know how many seasons it had counted. I did not know how many were left.

But I knew that I was part of it.

I walked home, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*The season has passed. The lamp burned, and the bell came, and the house kept itself through the season.*

*I watched it differently this year.*

*The lamp is part of the count. The bell is part of the count. The house is part of the count. The medallion is part of the count. The stones are part of the count.*

*I do not know what the count is counting.*

*But I know that I am part of it.*

*And I know that the count is older than the keeping.*

*I will learn what it is counting.*

*The season has passed.*

*And the count has turned.*

CHAPTER 115 â€” THE QUESTION

The winter came, and the town settled into its stillness, and I kept the account.

I had been keeper for four years. I had been a hunter since the clock in the market. I had worked with the society for a year. I had read the book of the first keeper, and I had found the tally, and I had understood that the boundary was a count.

I could not yet tell what the count was counting.

I carried the question through the winter. I carried it to the boundary, and to the chamber, and to the tower, and to the reading room above the chandler's shop. I carried it with the medallion warm against my chest.

I did not find the answer.

---

On a night in January, I went to the reading room.

I had not been there since the autumn. I had not been called. The society had not asked anything of me since the book, and I had not asked anything of them.

I went because I wanted to think.

The room was as I had left it â€” bare, with a single lamp on a table in the middle of the floor. The key to the reading room was in my pocket, and I set it on the table, and I sat in the chair, and I thought.

I thought about the count.

I had read the book of the first keeper. I had counted the tally. I had walked the boundary, and the wall, and the chamber. I had worn the medallion, and it had warmed at every stone. I had watched the season pass.

I knew that the boundary was a count. I knew that the stones were counters. I knew that the medallion was the counter. I knew that the count was older than the keeping.

I did not know what it was counting.

I sat in the reading room, in the lamplight, and I asked myself the question I had been avoiding.

What if the count was not counting the keeping?

What if the count was counting the keeper?

---

I had not asked myself that question before.

I had assumed the count was counting the keeping â€” the years, the seasons, the turning of the boundary.

But the first keeper had written that the tally was the count of the years. One mark for each year, kept since the first year.

And the count was more than the years of the keeping as I knew it.

What if the count was counting something else?

What if it was counting the keepers?

I sat in the reading room, in the lamplight, and I turned the question over in my mind.

The first keeper. Aurelius. The keeper before Vale. Vale. And me.

Five keepers, in the keeping I knew.

The count was more than five.

I did not know how many marks the tally had. I had counted them, and I had lost the number in the counting, and I had not written it down, because I had not understood what it was counting.

I had not written the number down.

I sat in the reading room, and I understood that I had made a mistake.

I had counted the tally. I had known the number. And I had not written it down, because I had not understood what it meant.

Now I needed the number.

I went home.

I took out the book of the first keeper.

I opened it to the first page. I looked at the tally.

I counted the marks.

I wrote the number in the red book.

And then I sat in the dark, and I looked at the number, and I understood that I did not know what it meant.

But I knew that I needed to find out.

---

I went to the Academy the following morning.

Daniel was there, in the room at the top of the stair, with his papers and his ledgers.

I set my notebook on his table. I opened it to the page where I had written the number.

"Count this," I said. "Tell me what it means."

Daniel looked at the number. He looked at me.

"It is a number," he said. "It is a large number. It is the sort of number a man writes when he has been counting something for a very long time."

"I need to know what it is counting," I said.

Daniel was silent a moment.

"Then you will need the records," he said. "The records of the counting. And the records of the counting are not in the Academy."

"Where are they?"

Daniel looked at me.

"The records of the counting," he said, "are in the place the counting was made. And I do not know where that is."

I stood in the room at the top of the stair.

The records of the counting were in the place the counting was made.

And no one knew where that was.

I took my notebook. I folded it. I put it in my coat.

I thanked Daniel. I left the Academy.

I walked home through the streets, with the number in my pocket, and I understood that the hunt was not over.

I had found the tally. I had counted the marks. I had written the number.

But I did not know what the number meant.

And I did not know where the counting had been made.

I walked home, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*I have counted the tally. I have written the number.*

*I do not know what it means.*

*The records of the counting are in the place the counting was made. And no one knows where that is.*

*I will find the place.*

*I will learn the count.*

*The hunt is not over.*

CHAPTER 116 â€” THE PLACE OF THE COUNTING

I searched for the place of the counting through the winter.

I could not yet tell what I was looking for. I knew only that the records of the counting were in the place the counting was made, and that the place was not the Academy, and not the vestry, and not the chamber beneath Bellweather House.

I searched the records. I searched the maps. I searched the old books, and the old letters, and the old papers.

I looked for a place older than the town â€” a place where the first keeper might have made the count, before the boundary was set, before the sign was carved, before the house was built.

I did not find it.

---

On a night in February, I went to the reading room.

I had not been called. I had not been asked. I went because I wanted to think.

The room was as I had left it â€” bare, with a single lamp on a table. The key to the reading room was in my pocket, and I set it on the table, and I sat in the chair, and I thought.

I thought about the count.

I had read the book of the first keeper. I had counted the tally. I had written the number. I had searched for the place of the counting, and I had not found it.

I did not know where the first keeper had made the count.

I sat in the reading room, in the lamplight, and I looked at the room.

The room above the chandler's shop. The room that had been locked for seventeen years. The room where the society met.

I had never asked what the room had been, before the society had taken it.

I stood.

I looked at the walls. I looked at the floor. I looked at the room.

It was bare. There was nothing in it but the table, the chair, and the lamp.

I knelt. I looked at the floorboards.

They were old. They were worn. They were the floorboards of a room that had been used for a long time, and then locked for seventeen years.

I ran my hand across them.

And I found the mark.

It was on the floorboard beneath the table, hidden, almost invisible â€” a mark worn smooth by years of being stood on.

A circle.

Three lines through it.

A single dot.

The first sign.

I knelt, in the lamplight, and I looked at the first sign on the floorboard.

The first sign. The mark of the maker.

On the floor of the reading room.

I did not know what it meant.

But I knew that I had found something.

---

I went to see Adeline Brume.

She was at the lending library, at her table, as if she had been waiting for me.

"The reading room," I said. "The room above the chandler's shop. What was it, before the society took it?"

Adeline looked at me a long moment.

"Why do you ask?"

"Because there is a first sign on the floorboard beneath the table," I said. "The mark of the maker. And I need to know what the room was, before the society took it."

Adeline was silent.

"The room was not always the society's," she said. "It was taken, seventeen years ago, from a man who had used it for a purpose the society did not ask about."

"What purpose?"

"I do not know," she said. "The man died, and the room was locked, and the society took it, and no one asked what it had been used for."

"The man," I said. "Who was he?"

Adeline was silent a moment.

"His name was Ferrand," she said. "Marcus Ferrand."

I stood very still.

Marcus Ferrand.

The constable before Vale. The man who had disappeared from the records. The name I had been given in the third test.

"Marcus Ferrand," I said. "The constable before Vale."

"He was a constable," Adeline said. "He was also a member of the society. He used this room. And when he died, the room was locked, and the society took it."

I stood at the table.

The third test had been a name. The name had been Marcus Ferrand. I had looked for him, and I had found nothing, and I had concluded that he had been deliberately removed from the records.

And now I had found that he had used the reading room.

The room with the first sign on the floorboard.

Marcus Ferrand had used the room where the first sign was.

I did not know what that meant.

But I knew that it was connected.

---

I went back to the reading room that night.

I knelt on the floor. I looked at the first sign on the floorboard.

The mark of the maker.

On the floor of the room Marcus Ferrand had used.

I did not know why it was there. I did not know what Marcus Ferrand had used the room for. I did not know whether he had made the mark, or found it, or kept it.

But I knew that the room was connected to the count.

The first sign on the floorboard. The first sign on every stone. The first sign on the floor of the chamber. The first sign on the medallion.

The first sign was everywhere.

And the first sign was the language of the count.

I knelt in the reading room, in the lamplight, and I understood that I had found a piece of the place of the counting.

Not the whole place. Not the beginning.

But a piece.

And I would find the rest.

I went home, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*There is a first sign on the floorboard of the reading room. The room was used by Marcus Ferrand â€” the constable before Vale, the man who disappeared from the records.*

*I do not know what the room was used for.*

*But I know that it is connected to the count.*

*The first sign is everywhere. The first sign is the language of the count.*

*I will find the place of the counting.*

*I will find Marcus Ferrand.*

*And I will learn what the count is counting.*

CHAPTER 117 â€” THE CONSTABLE BEFORE VALE

I looked for Marcus Ferrand again.

I had looked for him once before, in the third test, and I had found nothing â€” no trace, no grave, no record after the year of his retirement. I had concluded that he had been deliberately removed from the records.

I had been right.

But now I knew that he had used the reading room. I knew that he had been a member of the society. I knew that he had been the constable before Vale.

I went to see Calloway.

He was in his office, at his desk, and he looked up when I came in.

"You have been asking about Ferrand again," he said.

"I have."

"I thought you had finished with that name."

"I had," I said. "Then I found something. He used a room above the chandler's shop. A room with a mark on the floorboard. A room the society took after his death."

Calloway was silent a long moment.

"The room," he said slowly. "The room above the chandler's shop. I have known it was there for years. I could not yet tell Ferrand used it."

"He was a constable," I said. "He was also a member of a society. And he used that room."

Calloway looked at me.

"How do you know he was a member of a society?"

"Because the society took the room after his death," I said. "And because I was given his name, in a test, by the society."

Calloway stood.

He went to the cabinet at the back of the office. He took out a key. He unlocked a drawer that I had not seen opened before.

He took out a box.

"This is the box of the constables," he said. "It was passed to me by Vale, and to Vale by Ferrand, and to Ferrand by the man before him. It holds the things that are not in the records â€” the things that the constable keeps because they cannot be written down."

He set the box on the desk.

"I have not opened it," he said. "Vale told me not to open it, unless there was a reason. I think you have given me a reason."

He opened the box.

Inside, on a bed of dark cloth, lay a notebook.

Not the red book. Not the book of the first keeper. A different notebook â€” old, bound in dark leather, its pages yellow with age.

Calloway did not touch it.

"It is Ferrand's," he said. "It was in the box when Vale passed it to me. I have never opened it."

I looked at the notebook.

I reached out. I took it.

It was heavy in my hands.

I opened it.

The first page was not a page of words. It was a page of marks.

The same marks as the tally.

Rows of marks, in the same hand, running across the page.

I stood very still.

The tally from the book of the first keeper.

The marks from the oldest book of the Academy.

And now the same marks, in Ferrand's notebook.

"The marks," Calloway said. "You know them."

"I know them," I said. "There is a page of the same marks in the book of the first keeper. The first keeper wrote that they are the count of the years."

"And Ferrand kept them too," Calloway said.

I looked at the notebook.

Ferrand had kept the count.

The constable before Vale had kept the same count that the first keeper had kept.

I did not know what that meant. But I knew that it was connected.

---

I read Ferrand's notebook that night.

It was not the account of the first keeper. It was not the record of the boundary. It was the notebook of a constable â€” a man who had watched the town for fifteen years, and who had kept the things that could not be written down.

He had kept the count.

Page after page, the marks ran on, in the same hand, one mark for each year, kept since the first year.

And among the marks, in the margins, he had written notes.

*The count stands at the same number. It has not changed in my time.*

*The stones do not move. The count does not change. The boundary holds.*

*I do not know what the count is counting. But I know that it is the same count that the keeper before me kept, and the keeper before him.*

I read the notes.

Ferrand had kept the count. He had watched it. He had written that it had not changed in his time.

And he had written that he did not know what it was counting.

I turned the pages. The marks ran on. The notes grew fewer.

And on the last page of the notebook, there was a single line, in a hand that had grown tired with years:

*I have kept the count. I have not understood it. But I have kept it, the way the keepers before me kept it, and the way the keeper who comes after will keep it.*

*The count is the thing. And the keeper is not.*

I closed the notebook.

I sat in the dark.

The count was the thing. And the keeper was not.

The same words the first keeper had written. The same words I had written in the red book.

Ferrand had kept the count. He had not understood it. And he had kept it, the way the keepers before him had kept it.

And he had written that the keeper who comes after would keep it too.

I was the keeper who came after.

I sat in the dark, with Ferrand's notebook before me, and I understood that I was not the first keeper to wonder what the count was counting.

And I would not be the last.

But I would find out.

I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*Ferrand kept the count. He kept it for fifteen years. He did not understand it, and he kept it, the way the keepers before him kept it.*

*He wrote: "The count is the thing. And the keeper is not."*

*I am the keeper who came after.*

*I have kept the count. I have not understood it.*

*But I will find out what it is counting.*

*I have Ferrand's notebook. I have the tally. I have the marks of the Academy. I have the first sign.*

*And I will learn the count.*

CHAPTER 118 â€” THE NUMBER

I carried the three tallies with me â€” the tally from the book of the first keeper, the marks from the oldest book of the Academy, and the marks from Ferrand's notebook.

They were the same marks, in the same hand, the same count.

I had counted them many times. I had written the number in the red book. I had not understood it.

I set the three books on the desk, side by side, and I looked at them.

The same marks. The same hand. The same count.

Three books, three tallies, one number.

I could not yet tell what the number was counting.

But I knew that it was important.

---

I went to see Ysabel.

She was at her house near the river, and I set the three books on her table.

"Three tallies," I said. "The book of the first keeper. The oldest book of the Academy. Ferrand's notebook. The same marks. The same hand. The same count."

Ysabel looked at them for a long time.

"The first keeper made the tally," she said. "The boundary was set by the first keeper. The Academy book records the boundary as it was first set. And Ferrand kept the same count, fifteen years, without understanding it."

"Yes."

"There is a number," Ysabel said. "A number that has been kept by every keeper, from the first to the present. A number that has not changed in Ferrand's time. A number that the boundary is set to mark."

"Yes."

Ysabel was silent a moment.

"Then the number is not a count of years," she said. "A count of years would grow. Ferrand wrote that the count had not changed in his time. A count that does not change is not a count of years."

I stood in her house.

"A count that does not change," I said. "A count of what?"

"I do not know," she said. "But if the count does not change, then it is not counting time. It is counting something that is fixed â€” something that has always been the same number, and will always be the same number."

I looked at the three tallies.

A count that did not change. A count of something fixed.

I did not know what it was counting.

But I knew that it was not counting years.

---

I went back to the boundary.

I walked the line of stones, from the eastern marker to the corner where the wall turned. I counted the stones.

The number of stones was not the tally.

I stood at the fourth stone, where the hairline mark ran across its face.

The stones did not change. The count did not change. The boundary held.

I knelt at the fourth stone. I looked at the first sign at its base.

The first sign. The mark of the maker.

I looked at the hairline mark across the face.

Two marks on the same stone.

I had watched the hairline mark appear, in the first year. It had appeared, and it had not grown, and I had not known what it meant.

I looked at the hairline mark now.

It had not grown. It had not changed. It was the same mark it had been in the first year.

A mark that did not change.

I stood.

I looked at the line of stones.

The stones did not change. The count did not change. The boundary held.

And the hairline mark did not change.

I did not know what that meant.

But I understood that the count and the boundary and the stones were all the same thing â€” a thing that did not change.

A thing that was fixed.

And I was part of it.

---

I went home.

I sat at my desk.

I opened the red book. I wrote:

*The tally does not change. Ferrand wrote that the count had not changed in his time. The stones do not change. The boundary holds. The hairline mark does not change.*

*The count is not a count of years. It is a count of something fixed â€” something that has always been the same number, and will always be the same number.*

*I do not know what it is counting.*

*But I know that it is fixed.*

*And I know that I am part of it.*

I closed the red book.

I sat in the dark.

The count was fixed. The boundary was fixed. The stones were fixed.

And I was part of it.

I did not know what the count was counting.

But I would learn.

The hunt had led me to the number.

And the number was leading me somewhere.

CHAPTER 119 â€” THE REALIZATION

I did not sleep that night.

I lay in the dark, with the number in my mind â€” the fixed number, the count that did not change, the count that the boundary was set to mark.

A count that did not change. A count of something fixed.

I turned it over in my mind. I could not let it go.

The stones did not change. The count did not change. The boundary held.

And the hairline mark on the fourth stone did not change.

I had watched the hairline mark appear, in the first year. It had appeared, and it had not grown, and I had not known what it meant.

A mark that appeared, and did not grow, and did not change.

I sat up in the dark.

The mark appeared.

The mark appeared, and it did not grow, and it did not change.

What if the mark was not a crack?

What if the mark was a tally?

A single mark. One mark. Added to a count.

I had watched the hairline mark appear. I had written in the red book that the fourth stone had cracked, that a line had appeared across its face, too straight to be natural.

What if it was not a crack?

What if it was a mark â€” a mark added to the stone, the way a tally-mark is added to a count?

I stood.

I lit the lamp. I took out the book of the first keeper. I opened it to the first page.

I looked at the tally.

Rows of marks. Groups of marks.

A count that did not change.

And on the fourth stone, a mark that had appeared.

I could not yet tell what it meant. But I understood that the mark on the fourth stone and the tally in the book of the first keeper might be the same kind of thing.

Marks.

Tally-marks.

A count.

---

I went to the boundary before first light.

I knelt at the fourth stone. I looked at the hairline mark across its face.

A single mark. Straight, too straight to be natural.

I put my finger to it.

It was smooth. It was not a crack. It was a mark â€” a mark cut into the stone, or pressed into it, or made by some means I did not understand.

A mark that had not been there in the first year, and had appeared, and had not grown, and had not changed.

I stood.

I walked the line of stones. I looked at every stone.

The stones were old. They were worn. They carried the first sign at their base, and they carried the marks of the years â€” the cracks, the stains, the wear of weather and time.

And on the fourth stone, a mark that had appeared.

I did not know what it meant.

But I understood that the boundary was not only a count.

It was a tally.

And the tally was being kept.

---

I went to the chamber beneath Bellweather House.

I had not been there since the winter. I had not needed to go. But I went now, with the realization in my mind, and I knelt at the trapdoor and opened it.

I went down into the passage.

The first signs marked the way. I walked past them, to the chamber.

The first sign was on the floor.

I knelt.

I looked at the first sign on the floor.

The centre of the count.

I had set the medallion in it once, and I had felt the place beneath.

I looked at the first sign now.

The circle. The three lines. The dot.

And at the centre of the sign, where the lines crossed, there was a mark.

A small mark.

A single tally-mark, cut into the stone, at the centre of the first sign.

I had not seen it before. I had not looked for it. I had set the medallion in the sign, and I had felt the warmth, and I had not looked at the centre of the sign.

I looked at it now.

A single mark, at the centre of the first sign, on the floor of the chamber.

The same kind of mark as the hairline mark on the fourth stone.

A tally-mark.

I knelt, in the dark, in the lamplight, and I looked at the mark, and I understood.

The chamber was the centre of the count.

The first sign on the floor was the tally.

And the mark at the centre was a count â€” a count that was being kept, one mark at a time, by someone, or something, that I did not understand.

I did not know how many marks had been made. I did not know how many were left.

But I knew that I was part of it.

I stood.

I left the chamber. I walked back along the passage, up the ladder, and out of the house.

I walked home through the streets, and I sat at my desk, and I opened the red book, and I wrote:

*The hairline mark on the fourth stone is not a crack. It is a mark â€” a tally-mark.*

*There is a mark at the centre of the first sign on the floor of the chamber. The same kind of mark.*

*The boundary is not only a count. It is a tally. And the tally is being kept.*

*I do not know who is keeping it. I do not know how many marks have been made. I do not know how many are left.*

*But I know that I am part of it.*

*The keeper is part of the count.*

*And the count is being kept.*
