CHAPTER 120 â€” THE KEEPER OF THE TALLY

I could not yet tell who was keeping the tally.

I had found the marks â€” the hairline mark on the fourth stone, the mark at the centre of the first sign on the floor of the chamber. Marks that had appeared, and that had not grown, and that had not changed.

Marks that were being kept.

I did not know who was keeping them.

I sat at my desk, with the book of the first keeper before me, and I thought about the tally.

The first keeper had made the tally. He had kept the count. He had written that the tally was the count of the years.

But the count did not change.

A count that did not change was not a count of years.

And the marks on the stones â€” the marks that had appeared, and that had not grown, and that had not changed â€” were being added to something.

I did not know what.

I opened the book of the first keeper. I looked at the first page.

The tally. Rows of marks. Groups of marks.

And at the end of the tally, on the last page of the book, the sentence I had read many times: *I have kept my word. I have kept the keeping. I have erased myself so that the keeping will be kept for its own sake, and not for mine.*

I read the sentence again.

And I understood that I had been reading it wrong.

*I have erased myself.*

The first keeper had erased his own history. He had removed his name from the records. He had left no trace of who he was.

But he had left the tally.

The tally was not a count of years. It was a count of something else â€” something that the first keeper had kept, and that the keepers after him had kept, and that I was keeping now.

I did not know what it was.

But I knew that it was being kept.

---

I went to see Silas Wren.

He was at his house on the lane past the mill, among the objects without history, and he let me in.

"The tally," I said. "The marks in the book of the first keeper. You know them."

Silas was silent.

"I know them," he said. "I have known them for years."

"What are they counting?"

He was silent a long moment.

"I do not know," he said. "I have collected the objects that were scrubbed of their origin. I have kept the book of the first keeper. I have looked at the tally, and I have counted the marks. And I have not known what they are counting."

I stood in his house, among the objects without history.

"No one knows," I said.

"No one knows," Silas said. "The first keeper made the tally. He kept the count. He erased his own history. And he left the tally, without explanation, without meaning, without anything but the marks themselves."

"The marks are being added to," I said.

Silas looked at me.

"The marks?"

"There is a mark on the fourth boundary stone," I said. "A mark that appeared in the first year. A tally-mark. And there is a mark at the centre of the first sign on the floor of the chamber. The same kind of mark."

Silas was very still.

"The tally is being kept," he said.

"It is being kept," I said. "Not by me. Not by any keeper I know. But it is being kept."

We looked at each other in the lamplight.

"Then there is someone keeping the tally," Silas said. "Someone who is adding marks to the count."

"Who?"

"I do not know." He looked at me. "But if the tally is being kept, then the count is not complete. And if the count is not complete, then the keeping is not finished."

I stood in his house, among the objects without history, and I understood.

The keeping was not finished.

The count was not complete.

And I was not the last keeper.

The tally was being kept by someone â€” or something â€” that I did not know.

And the count would continue until it was finished.

---

I walked home through the dark streets.

I sat at my desk. I opened the red book. I wrote:

*The tally is being kept. There are marks on the stones, and marks on the floor of the chamber. Marks that have appeared, and that have not grown, and that have not changed.*

*Someone is adding marks to the count.*

*I do not know who.*

*The keeping is not finished. The count is not complete.*

*I am not the last keeper.*

*The tally will continue until it is finished.*

I closed the red book.

I sat in the dark.

I was the keeper. I was a hunter. I was a man who worked with a society that had no name.

And I had found that the tally was being kept.

I did not know who was keeping it.

But I would find out.

The hunt had led me to the tally.

And the tally was leading me to the keeper of the count.



===== FILE: Chapter_121_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 121 â€” THE WATCHER

I searched for the keeper of the tally through the spring.

I walked the boundary. I watched the stones. I watched the chamber. I watched the reading room, and the tower, and the house at the end of the lane.

I was looking for someone who added marks to the count.

I did not find him.

The stones did not change. The marks did not grow. The count did not change.

I began to think that I had been wrong â€” that the marks had appeared, and that nothing more would be added, and that the tally was complete.

Then, on a night in April, I saw him.

---

I was at the boundary, at the eastern marker, on the night of the full moon.

I had gone to watch the stones, because the stones were old, and the full moon showed them clearly, and I wanted to see whether the marks had changed.

The eastern marker stood in its place, the first sign at its base, worn smooth by years.

I knelt beside it. I looked at the stone.

And I heard it.

A sound. A small sound. The sound of a mark being made.

I looked up.

A figure stood at the far end of the line of stones, at the corner where the wall turned.

It was dark. I could not see the figure clearly. It stood still, at the corner, in the shadow of the wall.

I stood.

I did not call out.

I walked toward the figure, along the line of stones, and the figure did not move.

I came closer.

The figure was a man. He wore a dark coat. He stood at the corner of the wall, with his hand on the stone.

He was not young. He was not old. He had the look of a man who had been standing there for a long time.

I stopped a few paces from him.

"Who are you?" I said.

He did not answer.

I came closer.

He turned.

I saw his face in the moonlight â€” a face I could not yet tell, lined with years, with eyes that had seen a great deal and had not spoken of most of it.

"You are the keeper," he said.

"I am."

"I have been waiting for you."

I stood in the moonlight, at the corner of the wall.

"You have been keeping the tally," I said.

"I have been keeping the tally," he said. "I have been keeping it for a long time."

"How long?"

He did not answer.

"The count," I said. "What is it counting?"

He was silent a moment.

"The count," he said, "is counting the keepers."

I stood very still.

"The count is counting the keepers," I said.

"The count is counting the keepers," he said. "One mark for each keeper, since the first keeper set the boundary. The tally is the record of the keepers."

I looked at him.

"There have been many keepers," I said.

"There have been many keepers," he said. "More than you know. More than the records show. The tally is the record of them all."

"And me?" I said.

"You are a keeper," he said. "Your mark is in the tally."

I stood in the moonlight, and I understood.

The tally was not a count of years. It was a count of keepers â€” one mark for each keeper, since the first keeper set the boundary.

And my mark was in the tally.

"Then the tally is complete," I said. "When the last keeper has kept, the tally is complete."

He looked at me.

"The tally is not complete," he said. "The keeping is not finished. There are marks to be added."

I stood very still.

"There are keepers to come," he said. "Keepers after you."

"How many?"

He did not answer.

"Who are you?" I said.

He was silent a moment.

"I am the one who keeps the tally," he said. "I have kept it since the first keeper. I will keep it until the last."

He turned.

He walked along the line of stones, into the dark, and I did not follow.

I stood at the corner of the wall, in the moonlight, and I watched him go.

The keeper of the tally.

The one who had kept the count since the first keeper.

And I understood that the keeping was not mine alone. It was older than me, and larger than me, and it would continue after me.

I walked home through the fields.

I sat at my desk. I opened the red book. I wrote:

*I saw the keeper of the tally tonight. He has kept the count since the first keeper. He will keep it until the last.*

*The count is counting the keepers. One mark for each keeper, since the first keeper set the boundary.*

*My mark is in the tally.*

*The keeping is not finished. There are keepers to come.*

*I am not the last keeper.*

*But I am a keeper.*

*And my mark is in the count.*



===== FILE: Chapter_122_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 122 â€” THE NUMBER OF KEEPERS

I did not tell anyone about the keeper of the tally.

I did not tell Ysabel. I did not tell Calloway. I did not tell the society.

I sat at my desk, with the number in my mind â€” the number I had counted from the tally, the fixed number, the count that did not change.

A count of keepers.

I had counted the marks. I had written the number in the red book. I had not understood it.

Now I understood it.

The number was the number of keepers.

The tally was the record of the keepers â€” one mark for each keeper, since the first keeper set the boundary.

And the number was the count of them all.

I took out the red book. I opened it to the page where I had written the number.

I looked at it.

The number was large. Larger than I had expected.

There had been many keepers.

I sat in the dark, with the number before me, and I understood that the keeping was older than I had believed, and larger than I had believed, and that I was one keeper among many.

I could not yet tell where I stood in the count.

I did not know how many keepers had come before me.

I did not know how many would come after.

But I knew that I was part of the count.

And I knew that my mark was in the tally.

---

I went to the chamber beneath Bellweather House.

I knelt at the trapdoor. I opened it. I went down.

The first signs marked the way. I walked past them, to the chamber.

The first sign was on the floor.

I knelt.

I looked at the first sign on the floor.

The circle. The three lines. The dot.

And at the centre of the sign, the mark.

A single tally-mark.

The mark of a keeper.

I had not known what it was. I had thought it was a mark that had appeared, and that had not grown, and that had not changed.

Now I understood.

The mark at the centre of the first sign was the mark of a keeper.

My mark.

I knelt in the chamber, in the lamplight, and I looked at my mark.

It was a small mark. A single tally-mark, cut into the stone at the centre of the first sign.

The mark of the keeper who had come.

I had been in the keeping for four years. I had kept the account. I had kept the boundary. I had kept the door.

And my mark was in the tally.

I knelt there, in the chamber, and I understood that I was part of something larger than myself.

The keeping was old. The keeping was large. The keeping would continue after me.

And my mark was in the count.

---

I did not know how many marks were in the tally.

I had counted them, and I had written the number, and I had not understood it.

Now I understood it, and I did not want to count again.

The number was the number of keepers.

There had been many keepers.

And I was one of them.

I sat in the dark, and I thought about the keepers who had come before me â€” the first keeper, who had set the boundary and made the tally; Aurelius, who had signed the agreement; the keeper before Vale, who had opened the door and stayed in the passage; Vale, who had kept the door closed for fifty years.

And I thought about the keepers I did not know â€” the keepers whose marks were in the tally, whose names were not in any record.

The count of the keepers.

I did not know where I stood in the count.

But I knew that I stood in it.

I was a keeper.

My mark was in the tally.

And the keeping would continue after me.

---

I went to the reading room that night.

I sat in the chair, in the lamplight, and I thought about the keeper of the tally.

He had kept the count since the first keeper. He would keep it until the last.

He was not a keeper himself. He was the one who kept the record of the keepers.

I did not know who he was. I did not know where he came from. I did not know how long he had been keeping the count.

But I knew that he was real.

I had seen him. I had spoken to him. He had told me that the count was counting the keepers.

I sat in the reading room, in the lamplight, and I understood that there were things about the keeping that I would never fully understand.

The keeper of the tally. The count of the keepers. The marks on the stones.

Things that were older than the town, and larger than the town, and that would continue after the town was gone.

I went home.

I sat at my desk. I opened the red book. I wrote:

*The count is counting the keepers. The number I counted is the number of keepers.*

*There have been many keepers. More than I know. More than the records show.*

*My mark is in the tally.*

*The keeping will continue after me.*

*I am a keeper among many.*

*But I am a keeper.*

*And my mark is in the count.*

CHAPTER 123 â€” THE SUCCESSOR

I could not yet tell who would keep after me.

I had understood that the keeping would continue after me. The keeper of the tally had said so: "There are keepers to come. Keepers after you."

But I did not know who they would be.

I sat at my desk, in the evenings, and I thought about it.

I had no family. I had no child. I had no one to pass the keeping to.

I had the red book, and the agreement, and the gear, and the medallions, and the key to the reading room. I had the account.

But I did not have a successor.

I did not know whether the keeper of the tally would choose one, or whether the keeping would find one, or whether I was meant to find one myself.

I did not know.

---

I went to see Ysabel.

She was at her house near the river, and she let me in, and I sat by her fire.

"I have been thinking about the keeping," I said. "About who will keep it after me."

Ysabel looked at me.

"You have no successor," she said.

"No."

"And you do not know who will keep after you."

"No."

She was silent a moment.

"The keeping found you," she said. "You did not choose it. Vale did not choose you â€” the keeping did. It found you, the way it found Vale, and the way it found the keepers before him."

"The keeper of the tally," I said. "He keeps the count. He knows the keepers."

"And he will know the keeper who comes after you," Ysabel said.

I looked at her.

"How?"

"Because he has kept the count since the first keeper," she said. "And the count is not complete. There are marks to be added. The keeper who comes after you is already known to him â€” already counted, though the mark has not yet been made."

I sat by her fire.

"The keeper who comes after me," I said. "Is already known."

"Already counted," she said. "The tally is the record of the keepers. The keeper who comes after you is one of them. His mark is in the tally, though he has not yet kept."

I did not know what to say.

The keeper who would come after me. Already counted. Already in the tally, though he had not yet kept.

I did not know who he was. I did not know when he would come. I did not know whether I would meet him, or whether he would come after I was gone.

But I knew that he existed.

And I knew that the keeping would pass to him.

---

I walked home through the dark streets.

I sat at my desk. I opened the red book. I wrote:

*The keeper who comes after me is already counted. His mark is in the tally, though he has not yet kept.*

*I do not know who he is. I do not know when he will come. I do not know whether I will meet him.*

*But I know that he exists.*

*And I know that the keeping will pass to him.*

*I have no successor. I have no family. I have no one to pass the keeping to.*

*But the keeping has a successor.*

*The keeper who comes after me is already counted.*

*I will prepare for him.*

*I will leave the account so that he may read it.*

*I will keep the keeping so that he may take it up.*

*And I will trust the keeper of the tally to bring him, when the time is right.*

I closed the red book.

I sat in the dark.

The keeping was old. The keeping was large. The keeping would continue after me.

And I would prepare for the keeper who came after.

I did not know who he was. I did not know when he would come.

But I would be ready.

I would leave the account.

I would keep the keeping.

And I would trust the count.

CHAPTER 124 â€” THE PREPARATION

I began to prepare the account.

I had written the red book full. I had written everything I had learned, everything I had felt, everything I had kept. The red book was the account of my keeping.

But it was not enough.

The keeper who came after me would need more than the account. He would need to understand the keeping â€” the boundary, the sign, the house, the agreement, the door, the bell, the medallions, the gear, the count.

I began to write a new book.

Not the red book. Not the account of my keeping. A different book â€” a book of instruction, a book for the keeper who would come after me.

I wrote what I knew.

I wrote about the boundary, and the stones, and the first sign. I wrote about the chamber, and the passage, and the door that was not a door. I wrote about the bell, and the tower, and the lamp, and the season. I wrote about the agreement, and the keepers who had kept it, and the tally, and the count.

I wrote it all down, as clearly as I could, so that the keeper who came after me would not have to begin from nothing.

I wrote for months.

---

In the autumn, I went to see Silas Wren.

He was at his house on the lane past the mill, among the objects without history, and he let me in.

"I am writing a book," I said. "A book for the keeper who comes after me."

Silas was silent a moment.

"The first keeper erased himself," he said. "He left no name, no history, no account of who he was. He left only the tally, and the keeping."

"I know."

"Vale left no account. He left the document, and the gear, and the silence. You have written the account. You have done what they did not do."

I stood in his house.

"And now you are writing for the keeper who comes after," Silas said.

"Yes."

He looked at me a long moment.

"Then you are doing what no keeper has done," he said. "You are leaving more than the keeping. You are leaving the understanding."

I could not yet tell what to say.

"The first keeper kept without understanding," Silas said. "Vale kept without understanding. You have understood â€” the boundary, the sign, the count, the keepers. And you are leaving that understanding for the one who comes after."

I stood among the objects without history.

"Will you help me?" I said.

"Help you?"

"With the objects," I said. "The objects that were scrubbed of their origin. The keeper who comes after me will need to understand them. I would have them recorded."

Silas was silent a moment. Then he nodded.

"I will help you," he said. "I have kept the objects for years. I will help you record them, so that the keeper who comes after will know what they are."

I stood in his house, among the objects without history, and I understood that I was not alone in the preparation.

Silas would help me. Ysabel would help me. The society would help me, in its way.

And the keeper who came after me would not have to begin from nothing.

---

I wrote for a year.

I wrote the book of the keeping. I recorded the objects with Silas. I walked the boundary with Ysabel. I kept the account, and I prepared the instruction, and I waited.

I did not know when the keeper who came after me would come. I did not know who he would be.

But I knew that I would be ready.

I wrote the last page of the book of the keeping on a night in the spring.

I sat at my desk, with the book before me, and I wrote:

*To the keeper who comes after:*

*I have written this book so that you will not have to begin from nothing. It is the account of the keeping as I have understood it â€” the boundary, the sign, the house, the agreement, the door, the bell, the medallions, the gear, the count.*

*I did not understand all of it. I do not think any keeper understands all of it. But I have written what I know, and I have kept what I could, and I have trusted the count to bring you here.*

*The keeping is the thing. The keeper is not.*

*You are the keeper now. The count has brought you. The tally is yours to keep.*

*Keep the account. Keep the boundary. Keep the door. Keep the bell. Keep the medallions. Keep the gear. Keep the count.*

*And when you have kept as long as you are meant to keep, write your own account, and leave it for the keeper who comes after you.*

*That is the whole of the keeping.*

I set down the pen.

I closed the book.

I put it in the drawer, beside the red book, and the agreement, and the gear, and the medallions, and the key to the reading room.

I sat in the dark.

I had written the book of the keeping.

I had prepared for the keeper who would come after me.

I did not know who he was. I did not know when he would come.

But I would be ready.

The keeping was old. The keeping was large. The keeping would continue after me.

And I had done my part.

I had kept the account.

I had prepared the instruction.

And I had trusted the count.



===== FILE: Chapter_125_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 125 â€” THE VISITOR

The spring came again to Ashford.

I had been keeper for five years. I had written the account of the keeping. I had prepared the book for the keeper who came after me. I had recorded the objects with Silas, and walked the boundary with Ysabel, and kept the count with the tally.

I had done what I could.

Now I waited.

I could not yet tell what I was waiting for. I could not yet tell whether the keeper who came after me would come in my time, or after I was gone. I could not yet tell whether I would meet him, or whether he would find the book in the drawer after I had left it.

But I waited.

---

On a day in June, a visitor came to my door.

I did not know him. He was young â€” younger than I had been when I came to Ashford, with a face that had not yet been marked by the town.

He stood at my door, in the afternoon light, with a bag at his feet and a look of a man who had come a long way.

"Mr. Ashcroft?" he said.

"I am."

He looked at me a long moment.

"My name is Daniel," he said. "Daniel Wren. I am the son of the clerk. I have come to ask you about the keeping."

I stood very still.

The son of the clerk. The son of the man who kept the books.

"I have been told," he said, "that you are the keeper. I have been told that you keep the account, and the boundary, and the door, and the bell. I have been told that you have written a book for the keeper who comes after you."

I looked at him.

"Who told you?"

"The keeper of the tally," he said.

I stood in the doorway.

The keeper of the tally had sent him.

The keeper who kept the count had brought the keeper who would come after me.

---

I let him in.

I sat him at the desk. I opened the drawer. I took out the book of the keeping â€” the book I had written for the keeper who would come after me.

I set it on the desk before him.

"The account of the keeping," I said. "I wrote it for you."

He looked at the book. He did not open it.

"The keeper of the tally told me that I would find it here," he said. "He told me that you had written it, and that you had been waiting for me."

I sat in the room.

I had been waiting for him. I had not known who he was. I had not known when he would come. But I had been waiting.

"I have been waiting," I said.

I opened the drawer again. I took out the gear â€” the gear that Vale had given me, the gear that had been carried by keepers since the first. I set it beside the book.

"This is the gear," I said. "It is the keeper's key. I have carried it for five years. Now it is yours."

He looked at the gear. He did not touch it.

I took out the agreement â€” the folded paper, yellow with age, signed by Aurelius Ashcroft. I set it beside the gear.

"And this is the agreement," I said. "It is the document of the keeping. It has been kept by the keepers since Aurelius signed it. It is yours now."

He looked at the three things on the desk â€” the book, the gear, the agreement.

"I am not ready," he said.

"Neither was I," I said. "No one is ready for the keeping. The keeping finds you, and you keep it, and you learn as you keep."

He looked at the book.

"I am the son of a clerk," he said. "I know the books. I know the records. I do not know the keeping."

"The keeping is not in the records," I said. "It is in the boundary, and the stones, and the sign, and the account. And it is in the book I have written for you."

He looked at the book a long time.

Then he reached out, and he took it.

I sat in the room, and I watched him hold the book of the keeping.

The keeper who would come after me.

He was young. He was not ready. He did not know the keeping.

But the keeper of the tally had brought him to me.

And the count had brought him.

I did not know what he would do with the keeping. I did not know whether he would keep it well, or whether he would keep it long.

But I knew that he was the one.

I had written the book for him.

And he had taken it.

---

I walked with him to the boundary that evening.

I showed him the stones. I showed him the first sign at the base of each stone. I showed him the fourth stone, where the hairline mark ran across its face.

"The tally," I said. "The count of the keepers. Your mark will be in it."

He looked at the stones.

"I do not understand," he said.

"Neither did I," I said. "I have been keeper for five years, and I do not understand all of it. But I have kept it, and I have written the account, and I have passed it to you."

I looked at him.

"You will understand, in time. The keeping will teach you. The stones will teach you. The account will teach you."

We stood at the boundary, in the evening light, and I looked at the stones, and I understood that I was no longer the keeper.

I was the keeper who had passed the keeping.

The count had brought the next keeper.

And I had done my part.

I stood at the boundary, with the young man beside me, and I looked at the stones, and I understood that the keeping would continue.

The count would go on.

And I had been part of it.

I went home.

I sat at my desk.

I opened the red book. I wrote:

*The keeper who comes after me came today. His name is Daniel Wren. He is the son of the clerk. The keeper of the tally brought him to me.*

*I have given him the book of the keeping.*

*I have shown him the boundary.*

*I have passed the keeping.*

*The count will continue.*

*I have done my part.*

*The keeping is the thing. The keeper is not.*

*I have been a keeper.*

*And I have passed the keeping.*



===== FILE: Chapter_126_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 126 â€” THE PASSING

I stayed in Ashford for a year after Daniel came.

I did not keep the keeping. That was his now. I had passed it to him, and he had taken it, and the keeping was his to keep.

But I stayed.

I watched him learn. I watched him walk the boundary, and read the book, and ask the questions that I had asked, and learn the answers that I had learned.

He was a good keeper. He was not ready, when he came, but he learned. He walked the stones. He kept the account. He watched the season, and the lamp, and the bell.

I did not interfere. I did not advise, unless he asked. The keeping was his now, and a keeper must learn by keeping.

But I was there.

And when he asked, I answered.

---

In the spring, he came to me.

"I have read the book," he said. "I have walked the boundary. I have kept the account. But I do not understand the tally."

I looked at him.

"The tally is the count of the keepers," I said. "One mark for each keeper, since the first keeper set the boundary."

"I have counted the marks," he said. "The number is large. There have been many keepers."

"There have been many keepers," I said. "More than the records show."

"And my mark is in the tally?"

"Your mark is in the tally," I said. "The keeper of the tally made it when you came."

He looked at me.

"The keeper of the tally," he said. "You have seen him."

"I have seen him," I said. "He has kept the count since the first keeper. He will keep it until the last."

Daniel was silent a moment.

"And when I am done," he said, "when I have kept as long as I am meant to keep â€” then the tally will be complete?"

"No," I said. "The tally is never complete. The keeping is never finished. There will be keepers after you, as there were keepers before me."

I looked at him.

"You will write your own account," I said. "You will leave it for the keeper who comes after you. And the count will continue."

We stood at the boundary, in the spring light, and I understood that I had done what I could.

I had kept the keeping. I had written the account. I had prepared the instruction. I had passed the keeping.

And the keeping would continue.

---

On a day in autumn, I decided to leave Ashford.

I could not yet tell where I would go. I had not been anywhere in five years. I had been the keeper, and the keeper stays.

But I was not the keeper now.

I had passed the keeping. The keeping was Daniel's.

I was free to go.

I went to see Ysabel. I told her I was leaving.

"Where will you go?" she said.

"I do not know," I said. "Somewhere I have not been. Somewhere I have not kept."

She looked at me a long moment.

"Will you come back?"

"I do not know," I said. "Perhaps. When the count calls."

She nodded.

"The count will call," she said. "It called you here. It will call you back."

I left Ashford on a grey morning.

I did not tell Daniel I was going. I did not want a farewell. I wanted to leave the way the first keeper had left â€” quietly, without ceremony, so that the keeping would be about the thing, and not about the man.

I walked out of the town by the north road.

I did not look back.

---

I walked for a long time.

I walked through towns I had not seen, and fields I had not crossed, and roads I had not walked. I carried the medallions â€” the one I had carried all my life, the first sign, and the one I had found in the kept house at the end of Bellweather Lane. I carried the key to the reading room. I carried the account of what I had learned, written in my own hand, in a new book that I had begun after the passing.

I did not carry the gear. I had passed it to Daniel, with the book of the keeping and the agreement.

I did not carry the red book. I had left it with Daniel, in the drawer, with the book of the keeping.

The account was his now.

I walked, and I looked at the world, and I understood that I was no longer the keeper.

I was a man who had kept the keeping, and who had passed it on.

I did not know where I would go. I did not know what I would do.

But I knew that I would be ready.

The count would call.

And when it called, I would answer.

I walked north into the morning. The keeping had changed hands, but not become simple. Behind me, Ashford kept its ordinary face; ahead lay the unanswered part of the count. I could leave the duty, not its consequence.

CHAPTER 127 â€” THE MARSH ROAD

I had never been north of Ashford.

I had come to the town from London, by the south road, and I had stayed in the town for five years, and I had kept the keeping, and I had passed it on.

Now I walked north.

I could not yet tell where the road would take me. I could not yet tell what I was looking for. I only knew that I had to walk, and that the walking was part of something I did not yet understand.

I carried the medallions â€” the one I had carried all my life, and the one from the kept house â€” and the key to the reading room. I did not carry the gear; I had passed it to Daniel, with the book of the keeping and the agreement. I did not carry the red book â€” I had left it with Daniel, in the drawer.

The account was his now.

I walked north through the fields, and the lanes, and the small towns, and the country that grew wilder as the days passed.

And on the fourth day, I came to the marsh.

---

It began gradually.

The road grew softer. The fields grew wetter. The hedges gave way to reeds, and the reeds to water, and the water to a landscape I had never seen before.

The marsh was not a place. It was a country â€” a country of water and grass and sky, where the land and the water had never quite decided where one ended and the other began.

The road ran through it, raised above the water, on a causeway of stone and earth that had been there for a very long time.

I walked the causeway.

The marsh lay on either side of me, flat and grey, stretching to the horizon. The water was shallow. The grass grew through it. The reeds stood in it, and the wind moved through them, and the sound was the sound of a place that had never been silent and never been loud.

I walked, and I watched the marsh, and I understood that I had come to a place that had its own rules.

The marsh was not a place to hurry through.

It was a place to be careful in.

---

On the second day in the marsh, I came to a village.

It stood at the edge of the water, where the causeway met the land, a small village of low houses with roofs of thatch and walls of mud and stone. It was not on any map I had seen. It was the kind of village that existed for the people who lived in it, and for no one else.

I stopped at the inn.

It was a small building, at the end of the village, with a sign that had been repainted many times. The innkeeper was a thin man with a face that had seen many seasons in the marsh.

"You are from the south," he said.

"I am."

"We do not get many from the south." He set a cup of something warm before me. "The road does not bring them. The road brings the marsh people, and the marsh people do not stay."

"I am looking," I said, "for a place."

"A place?" He looked at me. "There are not many places in the marsh. There is the village, and the water, and the places that used to be, and the places that might be."

"What places used to be?"

The innkeeper was silent a moment.

"There is an old house," he said. "Out in the marsh, beyond the reed-beds. It has been empty for as long as anyone remembers. The marsh people say it was not built by them."

"Who built it?"

"I do not know." He looked at me. "No one knows. It was there before the village, they say. It has been there longer than the marsh people have been here."

I stood in the inn, in the warmth, and I understood that I had come to the marsh for a reason I had not yet named.

There was an old house, out in the marsh, that had been there longer than the village.

I did not know what it was. I did not know why I had come.

But I knew that I would find it.

---

I walked out into the marsh the following morning.

The village fell away behind me. The causeway gave way to a track, and the track to a path, and the path to reeds and water and the flat grey sky.

I walked for hours.

The marsh was quiet. The wind moved through the reeds. The water lay still. There were no birds, no animals, no sign of life but the marsh itself.

And then, through the reeds, I saw it.

A house.

It stood on a patch of higher ground, in the middle of the marsh, its walls dark with damp, its roof sagging, its windows empty.

It had been there for a very long time.

I stopped.

I stood in the reeds, in the grey light, and I looked at the house.

And I felt it.

Not the warmth of the medallions. Not the cold of the key. A different thing â€” a stillness, a waiting, the sense of a place that had been kept for a long time.

I had felt such things before.

I had felt them in Ashford, at the house at the end of the lane, at the chamber beneath Bellweather House, at the door beneath the mill.

I did not know what this house was.

But I knew that it was connected.

I walked toward it, through the reeds, across the water, and I understood that the hunt had not ended when I left Ashford.

The count had called me north.

And the count was not done with me yet.

CHAPTER 128 â€” THE HOUSE IN THE MARSH

The house stood on a patch of higher ground, surrounded by reeds and shallow water.

I approached it slowly. The ground beneath my feet grew firmer as I came closer, and the reeds gave way to a clearing of packed earth and stone. The house stood in the centre of it, dark and silent, its walls stained with the damp of many years.

I stood at the edge of the clearing, and I looked at the house.

It was not a large house. It was a single story, low, with a roof of slate that sagged in the middle. The windows were empty. The door was closed.

I crossed the clearing.

I could not yet tell what I would find inside. I could not yet tell whether the house was connected to the count, or to the keeping, or to something else entirely.

But I knew that I had to enter.

I reached the door. I put my hand on the latch.

It was not locked.

I pushed it open.

The air inside was cold. Not the cold of the marsh â€” the cold of a place that had been closed for a long time, the cold of still air and dark rooms and years that had passed without disturbance.

I stepped inside.

The room was bare. The floor was stone. The walls were plastered, and the plaster was cracked with age. There was no furniture, no pictures, no sign that anyone had ever lived here.

But there was a mark on the wall.

I crossed the room. I looked at the mark.

A circle.

Three lines through it.

A single dot.

The first sign.

I stood in the empty room, in the cold air, and I looked at the first sign on the wall.

The first sign. The mark of the maker. The same mark that was on the medallion, and on the stones, and on the floor of the chamber.

I had found it in the marsh.

I did not know what it meant. I did not know why the first sign was here, in a house in the middle of nowhere, in a place that was not on any map.

But I knew that it was connected.

---

I searched the house.

It was small. There was only one room, and a back room, and a narrow stair that led to a loft above. The loft was empty. The back room was empty.

There was nothing in the house but the first sign on the wall.

I stood in the back room, in the dark, and I thought about the first sign.

The first sign was on the medallion I had carried all my life. It was on the stones of the boundary. It was on the floor of the chamber. It was on the bell, and the clocks, and the key, and the box.

And now it was on the wall of a house in the marsh.

I did not know what it meant.

But I knew that I had been led here.

The count had called me north. The count had brought me to the marsh. The count had shown me the house.

And the first sign was on the wall.

I did not know what the house was. I did not know who had built it, or why, or when.

But I knew that it was part of the count.

I left the house.

I stood in the clearing, in the grey light, and I looked at the house, and I understood that I had not left the keeping when I left Ashford.

The keeping was not a place. It was a thing â€” a thing that was carried, and that called, and that would not be put down.

I had carried the keeping for five years. I had passed it to Daniel. But the keeping had not left me.

It had called me north.

And it had led me to the house in the marsh.

I did not know what I would find.

But I knew that I would follow.

The count had called.

And I would answer.

CHAPTER 129 â€” THE MARK OF THE MAKER

I did not leave the house in the marsh that night.

I built a small fire in the clearing, and I sat by it, and I looked at the house, and I thought about the first sign on the wall.

The first sign was the mark of the maker.

I had read that in the vestry book, years ago. The first sign was the sign of the maker, the sign that was set before the wall, before the boundary, before the Order.

I had carried the medallion all my life. I had found the first sign on the stones, and on the floor of the chamber, and on the bell, and on the clocks, and on the key, and on the box.

And now I had found it on the wall of a house in the marsh.

I could not yet tell what it meant.

But I knew that the first sign was everywhere.

And I knew that it was connected.

---

In the morning, I searched the marsh.

I walked out from the house, through the reeds, across the water, and I looked for other signs of the maker â€” other marks, other houses, other places where the first sign had been set.

I walked for hours.

The marsh was empty. There was no sign of the maker anywhere but in the house.

I came back to the house at midday.

I stood in the clearing, and I looked at the house, and I understood that I had been wrong.

The first sign was not the only thing the house had held.

I had searched the house. I had found nothing but the mark on the wall. But I had not looked beneath the house.

I knelt in the clearing.

I looked at the ground around the house.

The ground was packed earth and stone. It was the only firm ground in the marsh for as far as I could see.

I looked at the stones.

They were old. They were worn. They were set in the ground, in a pattern I had not noticed before.

A circle.

A circle of stones, around the house.

I stood.

I looked at the circle of stones.

The house stood in the centre of it. The stones were set in a ring, around the house, hidden by the reeds and the grass.

I counted them.

There were more than a hundred stones in the ring.

The same number as the boundary.

The same number as the tally.

I stood in the clearing, in the grey light, and I understood that I had not found a house in the marsh.

I had found the beginning of the boundary.

---

I knelt at the nearest stone.

I cleared the grass from its base.

The first sign was there.

The same mark. The same circle, three lines, dot. Cut into the base of the stone, hidden where the grass covered it.

I moved to the next stone.

The same sign.

The next. The next. The next.

Every stone in the ring carried the first sign at its base.

The same as the boundary in Ashford.

The same marks. The same signs. The same number of stones.

I stood in the centre of the ring, beside the house, and I understood.

The boundary in Ashford was not the only boundary.

There was a boundary here, in the marsh, older than the one in Ashford.

And the house stood at its centre.

I did not know what the house was. I did not know what the marsh boundary marked. I did not know why I had been led here.

But I knew that I had found something.

The beginning of the boundary.

The first sign.

The mark of the maker.

I stood in the ring of stones, in the grey light, and I understood that the hunt was not over.

The count had called me north.

And the count had led me to the beginning.

---

I did not leave the marsh.

I built a shelter beside the house, and I stayed, and I searched.

I walked the ring of stones. I counted them. I looked at the first sign on each one.

I searched the marsh beyond the ring. I looked for other signs, other houses, other marks of the maker.

I found nothing.

The ring of stones was the only sign of the maker in the marsh.

The house stood at its centre. The first sign was on its wall. The ring of stones circled it, each one carrying the first sign at its base.

And the count of the stones was the same as the count of the boundary.

I did not know what it meant.

But I knew that it was the beginning.

I sat by my fire, in the evenings, and I wrote in a new book â€” a book I had begun since leaving Ashford, a book of the journey, a book of what I was finding.

I wrote:

*The count has led me north. I have found a house in the marsh, with the first sign on its wall, and a ring of stones around it, each carrying the first sign at its base.*

*The count of the stones is the same as the count of the boundary in Ashford.*

*This is the beginning of the boundary. This is where the first keeper set the first sign.*

*I do not know what it means. But I know that I have found it.*

*The hunt is not over.*

*The count has led me to the beginning.*

CHAPTER 130 â€” THE CENTRE

I stayed in the marsh for two weeks.

I could not yet tell why I stayed. I had found the house, and the ring of stones, and the first sign on every stone. I had understood that this was the beginning of the boundary.

But I did not leave.

I walked the ring. I counted the stones. I looked at the first sign on each one.

And I looked at the house at the centre.

I had searched the house. I had found the first sign on the wall. I had not found anything else.

But I had not looked beneath the house.

The house stood on a patch of higher ground, in the centre of the ring of stones. The ground was packed earth and stone.

I had not looked beneath it.

I went to the house. I entered it. I knelt in the centre of the single room.

I looked at the floor.

It was stone. It was worn. It was the floor of a house that had been built a very long time ago.

I ran my hand across the stones.

And I found the mark.

It was on the central stone, beneath a layer of dust and age, nearly invisible â€” a mark worn smooth by years of being walked on.

A circle.

Three lines through it.

A single dot.

The first sign.

The same mark as on the floor of the chamber beneath Bellweather House.

I knelt, in the cold air, and I looked at the first sign on the floor of the house in the marsh.

The same mark. The same sign. On the floor of the house at the centre of the ring of stones.

I understood.

The house was not the centre of the ring.

The first sign on the floor was the centre.

And the ring of stones was set around it, each stone carrying the first sign at its base.

I knelt in the house, in the grey light, and I looked at the first sign on the floor.

The same mark as the chamber.

The same mark as the medallion.

The same mark as the stones.

And I understood that I had found the beginning of the count.

---

I did not move the stone.

I knelt in the house, and I looked at the first sign on the floor, and I did not move the stone.

I had learned, in five years of keeping, that the marks were not to be moved. The stones were set. The signs were carved. The count was kept.

I did not know what was beneath the first sign on the floor of the house in the marsh.

But I knew that I would not move the stone to find out.

I stood.

I left the house.

I stood in the clearing, in the grey light, and I looked at the house, and the ring of stones, and the marsh.

I had found the beginning of the boundary.

I had found the first sign on the floor of the house.

I had found the centre of the ring.

I did not know what it meant.

But I knew that I had found it.

And I knew that I would keep it.

I had come to the marsh because the count had called me. I had found the house, and the ring, and the first sign.

I had found the beginning.

And I would keep it, the way I had kept the boundary in Ashford.

I built my fire.

I sat by it, in the evening, and I wrote in my book:

*I have found the first sign on the floor of the house in the marsh. The same mark as the chamber beneath Bellweather House. The same mark as the medallion. The same mark as the stones.*

*The house is not the centre of the ring. The first sign on the floor is the centre.*

*I have not moved the stone. I will not move the stone.*

*I do not know what is beneath it.*

*But I know that I have found the beginning of the count.*

*And I will keep it.*

CHAPTER 131 â€” THE MARSH PEOPLE

I did not leave the marsh.

I built my shelter beside the house, and I stayed, and I kept the first sign on the floor of the house, and the ring of stones around it, and the count.

I could not yet tell how long I would stay. I could not yet tell whether the count would call me further north, or whether I had come to the end of the calling.

But I stayed.

The marsh people came to see me on the third week.

They came in a small boat, across the water, two of them â€” a man and a woman, in the dark clothes of the marsh. They beached the boat at the edge of the clearing, and they stood, and they looked at me.

"You have been here," the man said.

"I have."

"You have been to the house."

"I have."

They were silent a moment.

"No one goes to the house," the woman said. "It has been there as long as the marsh people have been here. It was there before the village. It was there before the marsh people came."

"I have been to it," I said. "I have kept it."

They looked at each other.

"Kept it?" the man said.

"I am a keeper," I said. "I have kept the boundary in Ashford. The count called me here. I have found the house, and the ring of stones, and the first sign on the floor of the house."

They were silent.

"The first sign," the woman said slowly. "You know the first sign."

"I have carried it all my life," I said.

She looked at me a long moment.

"We are the marsh people," she said. "We have kept the marsh for as long as anyone remembers. We have kept the water, and the reeds, and the paths. And we have kept the house."

"You have kept the house?"

"We have kept it," she said. "We have kept it empty. We have kept it closed. We have kept it the way it was given to us to keep it."

I stood in the clearing.

The marsh people had kept the house. They had kept it empty, and closed, and the way it was given to them to keep it.

And they had known that a keeper would come.

"We have been waiting," the man said. "We have been waiting for the keeper who would come to the house."

"For how long?"

"I do not know," he said. "Longer than the village. Longer than the marsh people have been here. There is a saying among us: the house will be opened when the keeper comes."

I looked at the house.

"The house is not locked," I said. "I entered it. I found the first sign on the floor."

"The house is not locked," the woman said. "It has never been locked. The keeping is not in the lock. It is in the knowing."

I stood in the clearing, and I understood.

The house had never been locked. The keeping had never been in the lock. It was in the knowing.

And the marsh people had kept it, not by locking it, but by knowing it â€” by keeping it empty, and closed, and the way it was given to them to keep it.

"You have come to the house," the woman said. "You have kept it. You have found the first sign on the floor. And you have not moved the stone."

"I have not moved it," I said.

"Then you are the keeper," she said. "You are the one we have been waiting for."

I stood in the clearing, in the grey light, and I understood that I had not come to the marsh alone.

I had come to a people who had been waiting for me.

And the keeping was not mine alone.

It was theirs as well.

---

I stayed in the marsh.

The marsh people taught me their ways. They showed me the paths through the water, and the ways of the reeds, and the signs of the weather. They told me the history of the marsh â€” the history that was not written down, the history that was kept in the mouths of the marsh people.

And they told me about the house.

"The house was built before the marsh," the woman said. "Before the water came. Before the land sank. It was built on the highest ground, and the marsh grew up around it, and the house stayed."

"Who built it?"

"I do not know," she said. "No one knows. It was there when the first of the marsh people came. It was given to us to keep, and we have kept it."

"The first sign," I said. "On the floor of the house."

"We have not entered the house," the woman said. "We have kept it empty, and closed, and we have not entered it. We have kept the knowing, and we have not entered the house."

I looked at her.

"Then I am the first to enter it," I said.

"You are the first," she said. "The keeper is the first. That is the keeping."

I stood in the marsh, in the grey light, and I understood.

I had come to the marsh. I had entered the house. I had found the first sign on the floor. I had not moved the stone.

And I was the first.

I did not know what that meant.

But I knew that I was part of the keeping.

The count had called me north.

The count had led me to the house.

And the house had been waiting for me.



===== FILE: Chapter_132_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 132 â€” THE KEEPING OF THE MARSH

I stayed with the marsh people through the autumn.

They taught me the ways of the marsh â€” the paths, the water, the reeds, the signs of the weather. They taught me how to read the marsh, the way I had learned to read the boundary in Ashford.

The marsh had its own keeping. It was a keeping of water and land, of the paths that changed with the season, of the places that were safe and the places that were not.

I learned it as I had learned the keeping of the boundary.

And I kept the house.

I went to it each day. I entered it. I knelt at the first sign on the floor. I did not move the stone.

I kept it.

I could not yet tell what I was keeping. I could not yet tell what the house was for. I could not yet tell why the first sign was on the floor.

But I kept it, the way the marsh people had kept it, and the way the keepers before me had kept the boundary.

I kept it.

---

In the winter, the marsh changed.

The water rose. The paths disappeared. The reeds died back, and the marsh became a different place â€” a place of open water and grey sky, where the land seemed to have vanished beneath the surface.

I stayed.

I did not go to the village. I stayed at the house, in my shelter, and I kept the house, and I kept the ring of stones, and I kept the first sign on the floor.

The marsh people came to me, in the winter, with food and dry wood.

"You are still here," the woman said.

"I am still here."

"You have kept the house through the winter."

"I have kept it."

She looked at me a long moment.

"The house has never been kept through the winter," she said. "No one has stayed in the marsh through the winter, since the marsh people came."

I stood in the cold.

"Then I am the first," I said.

"You are the first," she said. "You are the keeper."

I looked at the house, and the ring of stones, and the marsh.

I had come to the marsh because the count had called me. I had found the house, and the ring of stones, and the first sign on the floor.

I had kept them through the winter.

And I was the first.

---

The spring came, and the marsh changed again.

The water fell. The paths reappeared. The reeds grew, and the marsh became the place I had found, a year ago.

I had been in the marsh for a year.

I had kept the house. I had kept the ring of stones. I had kept the first sign on the floor.

I had not moved the stone.

I did not know what was beneath it.

But I had kept it.

And I understood that the keeping was not about knowing.

It was about keeping.

I had learned that in Ashford. I had learned it at the boundary, and at the door, and at the bell. I had learned it in the chamber, and at the reading room, and at the house at the end of the lane.

The keeping was not about knowing. It was about keeping.

I had kept the house in the marsh. I had kept it the way the marsh people had kept it, and the way the keepers before me had kept the boundary.

I had kept it.

And that was the whole of it.

---

On a day in the spring, I went to the village.

The innkeeper was there, at the inn, and he looked up when I came in.

"You have been in the marsh," he said.

"I have. I have kept the house."

He looked at me a long moment.

"The house," he said. "You have kept it."

"I have kept it."

He nodded slowly.

"Then you are the keeper," he said. "The marsh people have said so. The house has been kept through the winter. And you are the keeper."

I sat at the table.

"I am the keeper," I said. "I have kept the house, and the ring of stones, and the first sign on the floor. I have not moved the stone. I have kept it."

He looked at me.

"And what will you do now?"

I was silent a moment.

"I do not know," I said. "I have kept the house. I have kept the keeping. The count called me here, and I have come, and I have kept."

He nodded.

"Then the keeping is enough," he said. "The keeping is the thing."

I sat in the inn, in the warmth, and I understood that the keeping was the thing.

I had kept the house in the marsh. I had kept it through the winter. I had kept it the way it had been given to me to keep it.

And the keeping was enough.

I did not know what I would do next. I did not know whether the count would call me further.

But I knew that I had kept the keeping.

And that was enough.



===== FILE: Chapter_133_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 133 â€” THE CALLING

I stayed in the marsh for another season.

I had kept the house. I had kept the ring of stones. I had kept the first sign on the floor. I had not moved the stone.

I could not yet tell what I was waiting for.

The count had called me north. The count had led me to the house. The count had shown me the beginning of the boundary.

I had kept it.

And I had waited.

I did not know whether the count would call me further. I did not know whether I had come to the end of the calling, or whether there was more.

But I waited.

---

On a night in late summer, I felt it.

I was at the house, at the first sign on the floor, when I felt it â€” the warmth.

Not the warmth of the medallions. Not the warmth of the summer night. A different warmth â€” the warmth I had felt at the boundary in Ashford, at the door, at the chamber.

The warmth of the medallions.

I took out the medallion.

It was warm.

It had been warm many times. It had warmed at the stones, and at the door, and at the chamber. It had warmed at the first sign on the wall of the common house.

It was warm now.

I held it up.

I did not know why it was warm. I was in the house in the marsh, at the first sign on the floor. The medallion had warmed there before.

But it was warmer now.

I looked at the medallion. It was warm â€” warmer than it had been, warmer than it had ever been in the marsh.

And I understood.

The count was calling.

Not me.

The medallion.

The count was calling the medallion.

I stood in the house, in the dark, with the warm medallion in my hand, and I understood that I had not come to the end of the calling.

The count had led me to the house. The count had kept me at the house. And now the count was calling the medallion.

Calling it somewhere.

I did not know where.

But I knew that I would follow.

---

I left the marsh in the morning.

I went to the village first. I told the marsh people I was leaving.

"The count is calling," I said. "It is calling the medallion. I must follow."

The woman looked at me a long moment.

"Then you must go," she said. "The keeper follows the calling. That is the keeping."

I looked at the house, and the ring of stones, and the marsh.

"You will keep the house?" I said.

"We will keep it," she said. "We have always kept it. We will keep it until the keeper returns."

I stood in the village, in the grey light.

"I do not know when I will return," I said. "I do not know whether I will return."

"Then we will keep it," she said. "The keeping is the thing. The keeper is not."

I nodded.

I left the marsh.

I walked north, along the causeway, away from the water, and I did not look back.

The medallion was warm against my chest.

The count was calling.

And I would follow.

---

I walked north for a week.

The marsh fell behind me. The land rose. The fields returned, and the lanes, and the small towns.

I did not know where I was going. I did not know what the count was calling me to.

But the medallion was warm.

It had never stopped being warm since I left the marsh. It warmed against my chest, steady, constant.

I walked north, and I watched the medallion, and I waited for it to change.

On the seventh day, it changed.

I was walking through a small town, at midday, when the medallion grew warmer.

I stopped.

I looked at the medallion.

It was warm â€” warmer than it had been.

I looked at the town.

It was a small town, like many I had passed. Streets, houses, a market square, a church.

And at the end of the town, on a hill, there was a house.

I looked at the house.

It was large, old, set back from the town, behind a wall of old stone.

And the medallion was warm.

I stood in the street, in the midday light, and I looked at the house on the hill.

The count had called me north. The count had led me through the marsh, and away from the marsh, and to this town.

And the medallion was warm.

I did not know what the house was. I did not know why the count had brought me here.

But I knew that I would go to it.

The count was calling.

And I would answer.



===== FILE: Chapter_134_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 134 â€” THE HOUSE ON THE HILL

I walked toward the house on the hill.

The road led up from the town, past the wall of old stone, to a gate that stood open. Beyond the gate, a drive of packed earth led to the house.

I could not yet tell what the house was. I could not yet tell why the count had brought me here.

But the medallion was warm.

I passed through the gate. I walked up the drive.

The house was old. It was large, built of stone, with a roof of slate and windows that looked out over the town. It had been a house of some importance, once, and had fallen into a quieter age.

I came to the door.

It was closed.

I did not knock. I stood at the door, with the medallion warm against my chest, and I waited.

The door opened.

A woman stood in the doorway. She was older, in a plain dress, with a face that had seen many years. She looked at me for a long moment.

"You are the keeper," she said.

I did not deny it.

"You have come for the medallion," she said.

I stood very still.

"The medallion," I said. "The count has been calling it. It led me here."

She nodded.

"It has been waiting," she said. "It has been waiting for you."

She stepped back.

"Come in."

---

I entered the house.

It was quiet. The rooms were furnished, but plainly. The walls were lined with books, and papers, and the things of a long life.

The woman led me to a room at the back of the house, where a fire burned low.

There was a table in the middle of the room.

On the table, there was a box.

A wooden box, small, dark, plain.

The same size as the box that had held my medallion, all my life.

I looked at the box.

"Open it," the woman said.

I crossed the room. I opened the box.

Inside, on a bed of cloth, there was a medallion.

It was like mine. The same size. The same tarnished metal. The same cord.

And on its face, the same mark.

A circle.

Three lines through it.

A single dot.

The first sign.

I took it out.

It was cold.

I held it in my hand, and I looked at it, and I understood that I had found a third medallion.

The medallion I had carried all my life â€” the first sign, the mark of the maker, the medallion of my childhood. The medallion I had found in the kept house at the end of Bellweather Lane, that had warmed only for that house. And this medallion, in the box, in the house on the hill.

Three medallions. Three first signs.

"The count called you to it," the woman said. "The medallion has been waiting for you."

"Whose was it?" I said.

She was silent a moment.

"It belonged to the keeper before the first keeper," she said. "The keeper who came before the one who set the boundary. The keeper who made the first sign."

I stood in the room, with the cold medallion in my hand.

The keeper before the first keeper.

The one who had made the first sign.

"His medallion," I said.

"His medallion," she said. "It has been kept here, in this house, since his time. It has been kept until the keeper came for it."

I looked at the medallion in my hand.

The medallion of the keeper before the first keeper.

It was the third. I had carried one all my life. I had found the second in the kept house at the end of Bellweather Lane. And this was the third â€” the one that had been kept for the keeper who would come.

It was cold. My own medallion was warm. The second was warm, in my coat.

One warm from my own keeping. One warm from the kept house. And this one â€” cold.

But I knew that I had been led to it.

The count had called me north. The count had led me through the marsh, and away from the marsh, and to this town, and to this house.

And the count had led me to the medallion.

The medallion of the keeper before the first keeper.

---

I did not leave the house that night.

The woman gave me a room, and I stayed, and I looked at the medallion.

It lay on the table before me, cold.

My own medallion lay beside it, warm.

Three medallions. The first sign on each.

One warm. One cold.

I did not know what the difference meant. I did not know why my medallion was warm and the other was cold.

But I knew that they were connected.

I set the three medallions on the table. The first â€” the one I had carried all my life. The second â€” the one from the kept house. The third â€” the one from the house on the hill.

Three medallions. The first sign on each.

I looked at them.

The first was warm. The second was warm. The third was cold.

And I waited.

I did not know what would happen.

But I knew that something would.

I sat in the room, in the lamplight, and I watched the three medallions.

And in the middle of the night, the cold medallion warmed.

It warmed slowly, without touch, without cause â€” as if the other two, lying beside it, had reached out to it.

I watched it warm.

And I understood that the three medallions were meant to be together.

The keeper's medallion. The kept house medallion. The medallion of the keeper before the first keeper.

Three medallions. Three first signs.

And I was the keeper of all three.

I sat in the room, in the lamplight, and I looked at the three medallions, all warm now, side by side.

The count had called me to them.

The count had brought them together.

And I was the keeper of both.



===== FILE: Chapter_135_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 135 â€” THE KEEPER BEFORE THE FIRST

The woman's name was Eleanor.

She had lived in the house on the hill her whole life. Her family had kept the house, and the box, and the medallion, since the time of the keeper before the first keeper.

I sat with her, in the mornings, and I asked her about the medallion.

"Who was the keeper before the first keeper?" I said.

She was silent a moment.

"I do not know his name," she said. "No one knows his name. He was the keeper who came before the one who set the boundary. He was the keeper who made the first sign."

"The first sign," I said. "The mark on the medallion."

"The first sign," she said. "He made it. He carved it into the stone, and into the metal, and into the walls of the houses that would keep the keeping. He made the first sign, and he passed it to the keeper who came after him, and the keeper after him set the boundary."

I looked at the medallion.

"He made the first sign," I said. "And I carry it."

"You carry it," she said. "You have carried it all your life. You were carrying it before you knew what it was."

I was silent.

"The keeper before the first keeper," I said. "What happened to him?"

Eleanor was quiet a long moment.

"He was the last of the old keepers," she said. "He was the keeper who kept the keeping before the boundary was set, before the agreement was made, before the houses were built. He kept the keeping when there was only the first sign."

"And then?"

"And then he passed it on," she said. "He passed the first sign to the keeper who came after him, and the keeper after him set the boundary, and built the houses, and made the agreement. And the keeper before the first keeper â€” he did not keep anymore."

"He gave it up?"

"He gave it up," she said. "He gave up the keeping, and he kept only the medallion, and he kept the medallion until he died."

I looked at the medallion in my hand.

"And the medallion," I said. "It has been kept here, in this house, since his time."

"It has," she said. "My family has kept it. We have kept it empty, and closed, and the way it was given to us to keep it. We have kept it until the keeper came for it."

I stood in the room.

The medallion of the keeper before the first keeper.

Kept in this house, by this family, since his time.

Kept until the keeper came for it.

And I had come.

"The count called me here," I said. "The count led me to the medallion."

Eleanor looked at me.

"The count," she said. "You speak of it as if it were a thing."

"It is a thing," I said. "It is the count of the keepers. It is the tally. It is kept by a keeper who has kept it since the first keeper."

She was silent.

"You have seen him," she said.

"I have seen him."

She looked at me a long moment.

"Then you know," she said, "that the count is not finished."

I stood in the room.

"The count is not finished," I said. "There are marks to be added. There are keepers to come."

"And you," she said. "You have found the medallion of the keeper before the first keeper. You carry the first sign. You have kept the boundary, and the house, and the count."

She looked at me.

"You are more than a keeper," she said. "You are the keeper who came at the end of the old keeping, and the beginning of the new."

I could not yet tell what to say.

"Keep the medallion," she said. "Keep it with your own. They are meant to be together. The first sign was made twice, and the two are meant to be kept by one keeper."

I stood in the room, with the three medallions against my chest.

"I will keep them," I said.

She nodded.

"Then the keeping is whole," she said. "The first sign is kept. The medallion is kept. The keeper is kept."

I looked at her.

"The keeping is whole," I said.

"Go," she said. "The count is still calling. You have not come to the end of it."

I left the house on the hill.

I walked down the drive, past the gate, into the town.

I did not know where the count was calling me.

But I knew that I would follow.

I was the keeper.

I carried the first sign.

And I would follow the count to the end.



===== FILE: Chapter_136_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 136 â€” THE ROAD

I did not stay in the town.

I left the house on the hill, and I walked south, through the town, past the market square, past the church.

I could not yet tell why I walked south. The count had called me north. It had led me through the marsh, and to the house on the hill, and to the medallion of the keeper before the first keeper.

I had the third medallion now. I carried it with the other two, three medallions against my chest, the first sign thrice.

The count was still calling.

I walked south.

I did not know where I was going. I did not know what the count was calling me to.

But I walked.

---

The country changed as I walked south.

The land rose and fell. The fields gave way to woods, and the woods to fields, and the fields to villages, and the villages to towns.

I walked through each of them, and I did not stop.

I did not know what I was looking for. I did not know whether the count would show me a sign, or a house, or a person.

I walked.

The medallions were warm against my chest. All three of them. They had been warm since the night in the house on the hill, when the cold medallion had warmed beside the others.

I walked south, and the medallions were warm, and I followed the warmth.

---

On the third day, I came to a crossroads.

A signpost stood at the crossing. The arms pointed to towns I had not seen, and towns I had passed, and towns I did not know.

I stood at the crossroads, and I did not know which way to go.

I took out the medallions.

I held them in my hands.

They were warm.

I held them up, one in each hand, and I waited.

The medallions did not change. They did not grow warmer in any direction. They were warm, steady, the same warmth in every direction.

I stood at the crossroads, and I did not know which way the count was calling.

I put the medallions back in my coat.

I looked at the signpost.

The arms pointed to the north, and the south, and the east, and the west.

I did not know which way to go.

I stood at the crossroads, in the grey light, and I understood that the count was not calling me to a place.

The count was calling me to a choice.

I stood at the crossroads, and I chose.

I chose the south road.

I walked south.

I did not know why I chose the south. I did not know what the south held.

But I chose.

And I walked.

---

I walked south for a week.

The land grew warmer. The fields grew greener. The villages grew larger, and the towns grew busier.

I walked through them, and I did not stop.

The medallions were warm. They had not changed since the crossroads.

I walked south, and I did not know what I was looking for.

I did not know whether the count would show me anything.

I did not know whether I had chosen rightly.

But I walked.

On the seventh day, I came to a river.

It was a wide river, slow and deep, with a bridge of old stone crossing it.

I stood on the bridge, and I looked at the river.

The water was dark. The current was slow. The river moved as it had moved for a thousand years, without hurry, without pause.

I looked at the river, and I understood that I had come to the end of the road.

Not the end of the journey. The end of the road.

The road south ended at the river.

Beyond the river, there was another road.

I did not know whether I would cross.

I stood on the bridge, with the medallions warm against my chest, and I looked at the river, and the road beyond it.

I did not know what I would do.

I did not know whether the count wanted me to cross, or to stay, or to turn back.

I stood on the bridge, in the grey light, and I waited.

The medallions were warm.

I did not know what they were telling me.

I stood on the bridge, and I waited.

And in the waiting, I understood.

The count was not calling me to a place. It was calling me to a purpose.

I had found the house in the marsh. I had found the medallion of the keeper before the first keeper. I had kept the keeping.

And the count had called me to the crossroads, and I had chosen the south, and I had walked to the river.

And now I stood at the river, and I did not know whether to cross.

I stood on the bridge, and I waited.

And I understood that the waiting was the purpose.

I had been a keeper. I had been a hunter. I had been a man who worked with a society.

And now I was a man who stood at a river, waiting.

I did not know what I was waiting for.

But I knew that I would wait.

The count had called me.

I had come.

And I would wait until the count called again.



===== FILE: Chapter_137_EN.md =====

CHAPTER 137 â€” THE CROSSING

I waited at the river for three days.

I did not cross. I did not turn back. I stayed at the bridge, and I watched the river, and I waited.

The medallions were warm. They had not changed.

I could not yet tell what the count was telling me.

On the third day, a man came to the bridge.

He was old. He walked with a stick, and he carried a bag over his shoulder. He looked at me, and he looked at the river, and he looked at me again.

"You have been here a while," he said.

"I have."

"You are waiting to cross."

"I am waiting to know whether I should cross."

He looked at the river.

"The river does not care," he said. "It has been here for a thousand years. It will be here for a thousand more. It does not care whether you cross."

I looked at the river.

"I am not waiting for the river," I said. "I am waiting for the count."

He looked at me.

"The count," he said.

"The count of the keepers," I said. "It has been calling me. It led me to the marsh, and to the house on the hill, and to the crossroads. And now I am at the river, and I do not know whether it wants me to cross."

He was silent a moment.

"And if you cross?"

"Then I will be on the other side of the river."

"And if you do not cross?"

"Then I will be on this side."

He looked at me, and he nodded.

"Then the river has already decided," he said. "The river does not care. The count does not care. The count called you to the crossroads, and you chose, and you walked to the river. And now you are at the river, and the river does not care whether you cross."

He looked at me.

"You are the one who decides."

He walked past me, across the bridge, to the other side of the river.

I watched him go.

He walked down the road, on the other side, and he did not look back.

I stood on the bridge, and I understood.

The count had called me to the crossroads. I had chosen. I had walked to the river.

And now I was at the river, and the river did not care whether I crossed.

I was the one who decided.

I looked at the river.

I looked at the road on the other side.

I looked at the medallions against my chest.

I crossed the bridge.

I walked to the other side of the river.

I did not know what the south held. I did not know whether the count would call me again.

But I had crossed.

And I was on the other side.

---

I walked south for a month.

I walked through towns and villages, through fields and woods, through country I had never seen.

The medallions were warm. They had not changed.

I did not know what I was looking for. I did not know whether the count would call me again.

But I walked.

I walked until I came to the sea.

I stood on the shore, in the grey light, and I looked at the water.

The sea was wide. The waves moved, slow and steady, the way they had moved for a thousand years.

I stood on the shore, and I looked at the sea.

I had come to the end of the land.

There was nowhere further south.

I stood on the shore, and I waited.

The medallions were warm.

I did not know what the count was telling me.

I stood on the shore, and I waited.

And in the waiting, I understood.

The count had called me to the sea.

Not to cross it. Not to find anything.

To stand at the edge of it, and to understand that the calling was not about the destination.

The calling was about the journey.

I had walked from Ashford to the marsh. From the marsh to the house on the hill. From the house on the hill to the crossroads. From the crossroads to the river. From the river to the sea.

I had followed the count.

And I had come to the end of the land.

I stood on the shore, at the edge of the sea, and I understood that the count had called me to the sea.

I had come.

And the keeping was enough.

I stood on the shore, with the medallions warm against my chest, and I looked at the sea.

The count had called me.

I had come.

And I was the keeper.

I did not know what I would do next.

But I knew that I would keep.

The keeping was the thing.

And I was the keeper.
